BOOK VIII. Arrival of the Spaniards on the coasts of Anahuac. Disquiet, embassies, and gifts of King Moctezuma. Confederation of the Spaniards with the Totonacs;

their war and alliance with the Tlaxcalans: their severity toward the

Cholulans, and their solemn entry into Mexico. Account of the celebrated

Indian woman Doña Marina. Founding of Veracruz, the first colony of the

Spaniards.

he Spaniards, who as early as the year 1492 had discovered

T the New World under the guidance of the famous Genoese

Christopher Columbus, and within a few years had subjected the principal islands of the Antilles to the Crown of Castile, thereafter often set sail to discover new countries and to barter European trifles for American gold. Among others, in the year 1517 Francisco Hernández de Córdoba sailed from the port of Ajaruco (today § 1. Havana) with a hundred and ten soldiers, and steering westward on First voyages of the

Spaniards to the the advice of Antonio Alaminos, one of the most expert and most fa‐ coasts of Anahuac. mous pilots of that time, and then bearing to the south, at the beginning of March they discovered the eastern cape of the peninsula of Yucatán, which they called Cape Catoche. They coasted along a part of that country, admiring the fine buildings and the high towers which were to be seen along the coast, (a) the clothes of various colors worn by the Indians:

A2 objects

(a) Dr. Robertson says in bk. 3 that the Spaniards landed, and advancing into the country (of Yucatán) observed with astonishment great houses built of stone. So he speaks where he recounts the voyage of Hernández; but a few pages later, speaking of the voyage of Grijalva, he says: Many villages were scattered along the coast, in which (the Spaniards) could make out houses of stone, which at a distance appeared white and splendid. In the warmth of their imagination they fancied these to be so many cities adorned with towers

p. 4

objects never before seen by them in the New World. The Yucatecs, Bk. VIII.

for their part, marveled at the size, the form, and the equipment of

the ships. In two places where the Spaniards set foot on land they had

two skirmishes with the Indians, in which, and in other hardships

that befell them, they lost half their soldiers, and the captain himself

received twelve wounds, which within a few days caused his death.

But having returned in haste to Cuba, they revived with their report,

and with some gold which they brought as a sample, stolen from a

temple of Yucatán, the greed of Diego Velázquez, formerly conqueror

and then governor of that island; so that the following year he sent

his kinsman Juan de Grijalva with four ships and two hundred and

forty soldiers. This commander, after reconnoitering the island of

Cozumel, a few miles from the eastern coast of Yucatán, coasted the

whole of that country which lies from there as far as the river

Pánuco, bartering little glass beads and other such trinkets for the

gold which they so much desired, and for the provisions which they

needed.

When they arrived at that islet which they called San Juan de Ulúa,

(b) little more than a mile distant from

the

and with cupolas. Among all the historians of Mexico that I have read I have not

found a single one who says that the Spaniards imagined cupolas in Yucatán. This

fancy was Robertson's, not theirs. It did seem to them that they saw high towers and

very large houses, as indeed there were. The temples of Yucatán, like those of

Anahuac, were built for the most part in the manner of towers, and many were very

high. Bernal Díaz, a most sincere author and an eyewitness of all that befell the

Spaniards on those first voyages to Yucatán, where he speaks of the landing they

made on their first voyage on the coast of Campeche, says thus: They led us (the

Indians) to certain very large houses, very well built of stone and lime. So that they not only

saw the buildings from afar, but came up to them and went inside. Since, moreover,

the use of lime was so familiar to those nations, it is no wonder that the custom of

whitewashing houses was common to them as well. See on this point bk. VII of our

History. For the rest, I cannot understand how a house that is not in fact white can

look white from afar.

(b) They gave that islet the name of San Juan because they landed there

p. 5

the shore of Chalchiuhcuecan, the Mexican governors of those coasts, astounded at the sight of ships so large and of men of such Bk. VIII. strange appearance and bearing, consulted among themselves about what was to be done, and resolved to repair in person to the court to give the king an account of a thing so extraordinary; and, so as to give him a fuller idea of it, they had their painters represent after a fashion the ships, the artillery, the arms, the dress, and the appearance of that new people. Without delay they set out for the court, where they told the king by word of mouth all that was on the coast, and presented him with the paintings and some little glass beads which they had received from the Spaniards. Moctezuma was troubled at hearing news so strange; but, not to precipitate his decision in an affair of such moment, he held council with Cacamatzin, king of Acolhuacan, his nephew, with Cuitláhuac, lord of Iztapalapa, his brother, and with twelve other persons of rank, his ordinary councilors. After a long conference they concluded by common opinion that the one who had landed on those shores with so great an array could be no other than the god of the air, Quetzalcoatl, awaited by them for so many years; for there ran among those nations, as we have said elsewhere, an ancient tradition that this god, after winning the esteem and veneration of the peoples in Tollan, in Cholula, and in Onohualco by his innocent life and his singular beneficence, had vanished from there, having first promised them to return after some time to rule them in peace and make them happy. The kings of those countries considered themselves lieutenants of that god, and trustees of the crown, which they would have to yield to him

as

on the day of the holy Precursor, and because that was the name of the commander. They also called it Ulúa; for, having found there two human victims freshly sacrificed, and having asked by signs the reason for so inhuman a deed, the Indians replied, pointing toward the west, Acolhua, Acolhua, meaning to make them understand that they did it by order of the Mexicans, who, like all those of the Valley of Mexico, were called Acolhuas by the peoples distant from their capital. On this islet there is at present a good fortress to defend the entrance to the port of Veracruz.

as soon as he appeared there. This immemorial tradition, the various

Bk. VIII.

signs they observed in the Spaniards that agreed with what their

mythology related of Quetzalcoatl, the astonishing size of the ships

compared with that of their own boats, or canoes, and the noise and

violence of the artillery, so like those of the clouds, led them to be‐

lieve that the man who had arrived at their coasts with such a terrible

apparatus of lightning flashes, thunderbolts, and thunder could be no

other than the god of the air. Moved by this belief, Moctezuma or‐

dered five persons of his court to betake themselves at once to

Chalchiuhcuecan, to congratulate this supposed deity, in his name

and in that of the whole kingdom, on his happy arrival in that land,

and to bring him, as homage, a great gift; but before sending them off

he issued an order in advance to the governors of the coasts to post

sentinels on the high mountains of Nauhtlan, Cuauhtla, Mictlan, and

Tochtlan, so that they might observe the movements of the fleet, and

send prompt word to the court of everything that might happen. In

spite of their utmost diligence the Mexican ambassadors could not

overtake the Spaniards, who, having finished their trade on that

coast, continued their voyage hugging the shore as far as the river

Pánuco, and from there returned to Cuba with ten thousand sequins

in gold, partly acquired by the barter of trinkets, and partly from a

rich gift made to the commander by a lord of Onohualco.

The governor of Cuba was greatly vexed that Grijalva had not

§ 2.

Character of the planted a colony in that new country, which everyone represented as principal conquerors the richest and happiest in the world; so he promptly had another,

of Mexico.

larger armament fitted out, the command of which several of the

principal colonists of that island contended for; but the governor, on

the advice of two of his confidants, entrusted it to Hernán Cortés, a

man of noble birth and rich enough to bear, with his own capital and

the help of his friends, a good part of the expenses of the fleet. He

was born in Medellín, a small town of Extremadura, in 1485. On his

father's side he was a Cortés,

and

p. 7

and a Monroy, and on his mother's side a Pizarro and an Altamirano,

Bk. VIII. the blood of those four lineages, which were among the most illustrious and the oldest of that town, having been united in him. At the age of fourteen he was sent by his parents to Salamanca, so that by learning Latin and jurisprudence in that famous university he might be of use to his house, which had been reduced to poverty; but it was not long before his martial bent drew him away from his studies and carried him to the New World, in the footsteps of many illustrious young men of his nation. He accompanied Diego Velázquez in the conquest of the island of Cuba, where he acquired property and won great authority for himself. He was a man of great talent, very shrewd, courageous, and skillful in the exercise of arms, fertile in finding means and expedients to bring his projects to completion, extremely ingenious in making himself obeyed and respected even by his peers, magnanimous in his designs and in his actions, prompt in action, modest in speech, steadfast in his undertakings, and patient in his adversities. His zeal for religion was in no way inferior to his constant and inviolable fidelity toward his sovereign; but the splendor of these and of other good qualities, which raised him to the class of heroes, was eclipsed by some actions unworthy of the greatness of his mind. His unruly love of women entangled him in some evil practices, and in earlier times had brought upon him grave troubles and perils. Too great zeal, or obstinacy, in his undertakings, and the fear of upsetting his fortune made him at times fail in justice, in gratitude, and in humanity; but where was there ever a general and conqueror, formed in the school of the world, in whom the virtues did not stand in the balance against the vices? Cortés was of good stature, and of a well-proportioned body, robust and agile. He had a somewhat prominent chest, a black beard, and lively, amiable eyes. Such is the portrait of the famous Conqueror of Mexico left to us by the first historians, who knew him. (*)

As

(*) In the portrait of Cortés which we shall give below, that Conqueror is represented with a grim look, through the fault of the engraver.

p. 8

As soon as he saw himself honored with the office of general of the Bk. VIII. fleet, he set about making with the greatest diligence all the prepara‐

tions for the voyage, and began to treat himself as a great lord, both

in his bearing and in his household, well aware of the power that such

a display has to dazzle the common people and to win authority. He

at once raised the royal standard before his house, and had a procla‐

mation published throughout the island to enlist soldiers. Men of the

most conspicuous in that country, both for their birth and for their

offices, vied with one another in placing themselves under his com‐

mand, such as Alonso Hernández Portocarrero, cousin of the Count

of Medellín, Juan Velázquez de León, a close relative of the governor,

Diego de Ordaz, Francisco de Montejo, Francisco de Lugo, and oth‐

ers whom we shall name in the course of the History. Among all of

them particular mention is due to Pedro de Alvarado of Badajoz,

Cristóbal de Olid of Baeza in Andalusia, and Gonzalo de Sandoval of

Medellín, because they were the first commanders of the troops em‐

ployed in that conquest, and those who cut the most brilliant figure:

all three most courageous warriors, hardened to the labors of war and

skilled in the military art, but of very different character. Alvarado

was a well-made and very agile young man, fair-haired, graceful, mer‐

ry, affable, given to luxury and to pastimes, greedy for gold, of which

he had need in order to display grandeur, and, as the early authors af‐

firm, little scrupulous in the manner of acquiring it, and inhuman and

violent in some of his expeditions. Olid was heavily built, suspicious,

and double-dealing. Both served Cortés very well in the conquest; but

later they were ungrateful to him, and both met a tragic end.

Alvarado died in New Galicia, crushed by a horse that fell down a

mountain. Olid was beheaded by his enemies in the square of Naco in

the province of Honduras. Sandoval was a young man of good birth,

scarcely twenty-two years old when he enlisted in the army of his

compatriot Cortés. He was of proportionate stature and robust con‐

stitution, with chestnut, curly hair, a strong, gruff voice, of

few

Hernán Cortés. — Pedro de Alvarado.

Moctezuma Xocoyotzin.

Gonzalo de Sandoval. — Cristóbal de Olid.

p. 9

few words, and of outstanding deeds. To him Cortés entrusted the most arduous and dangerous expeditions, and in all of them he came Bk. VIII. off with honor. In the war against the Mexicans he was commander of one part of the Spanish army, and in the siege of the capital he had more than thirty thousand men under his orders, always earning by his good conduct the favor of his general, the respect of his soldiers, and the love of his very enemies. He founded the colony of Medellín on the coast of Chalchiuhcuecan, and that of Espíritu Santo on the river Coatzacoalco. He was commander of the garrison of Veracruz, and for some time governor of Mexico, and in all his employments he made his equity manifest. He was constant and assiduous in toil, obedient and loyal to his general, kind toward his soldiers, humane (*) toward his enemies, and entirely free from the common contagion of avarice. And, to put it in few words, I find in the whole series of the Conquerors no more accomplished man, nor one more worthy of praise, since there never

History of Mexico, Vol. III. B was

(*) Dr. Robertson lays at Sandoval’s door that frightful example of severity made upon the people of Pánuco, when the Spaniards burned sixty lords and four hundred nobles before the eyes of their children and relatives, and he cites the testimony of Cortés and of Gómara; but Cortés does not affirm that Sandoval inflicted that punishment, nor does he even name him. Bernal Díaz, whose testimony on this point is worth far more than Gómara’s, says that Sandoval, having defeated the people of Pánuco and taken prisoner twenty lords and some other persons of note, wrote to Cortés asking for his decision regarding the prisoners; and Cortés, to make their condemnation the more just, committed their trial to Diego de Ocampo, judge of that province, who, after hearing their confession, condemned them to be burned, as was done. Bernal Díaz does not state the number of the culprits punished. Cortés says that four hundred, lords and principal persons together, were burned. Such a punishment was without doubt excessive and cruel; but Robertson, who reproaches the Spaniards so much for it, ought, to make his impartiality manifest, to declare the motives those men had for venting their cruelty on the people of Pánuco. These, after submitting to the Crown of Spain, shook off the yoke and, running to arms, threw that whole province into turmoil; they killed four hundred Spaniards, of whom they burned forty alive in a house, and ate the others. These atrocious crimes are not enough to excuse the Spaniards; but they do make their severity less blameworthy. Robertson likewise read in Gómara both the misdeeds of the people of Pánuco and the severity of the Spaniards; but he exaggerates the latter and is silent about the former.

was among them anyone who knew so well how to reconcile youthful

Bk. VIII.

ardor with prudence, bravery and intrepidity with humanity, conti‐

nence with merit, and modesty with good fortune. He died in the

very flower of his age, in a place in Andalusia, while on the way to the

court of Spain together with Cortés. A man truly worthy of a better

fortune, and of a longer life.

Cortés’s fleet and When almost all the preparations for the voyage had been made,

voyage.

the governor of Cuba, at the suggestion and through the intrigues of

Cortés's rivals, revoked the commission already given to him, and

even gave orders to imprison him; but those who were charged with

the arrest did not dare to carry it out, seeing so many men of note

and courage committed to supporting the cause of their new general;

so that Cortés, who had not only spent his whole capital on the

preparations but had even gone into debt, kept his office in spite of

his enemies, and, having everything already in order, sailed from the

port of Ajaruco on February 10, 1519. The fleet consisted of eleven

ships, five hundred and eight soldiers distributed in eleven compa‐

nies, a hundred and nine seamen, sixteen horses, ten cannon, and four

falconets. They sailed under the direction of the pilot Alaminos as far

as the island of Cozumel, where they recovered Jerónimo de Aguilar,

a Spanish deacon, who some years before, while traveling from

Darién to the island of Santo Domingo, or Hispaniola, had been ship‐

wrecked on the coasts of Yucatán and made a slave by the Indians,

and who now, informed of the arrival of the Spaniards at Cozumel,

obtained his freedom from his master and joined the fleet. By long in‐

tercourse with the Yucatecs he had learned the Maya language, which

is spoken there; whence he received from Cortés the post of

interpreter.

§ 4. From Cozumel they advanced, coasting the peninsula of Yucatán,

Victory of the as far as the river of Chiapa in the province of Tabasco, up which, in Spaniards in Tabasco.

the boats and the smaller craft, they made their way some distance

into the country as far as a palm grove, where, having set foot on land

on the pretext of procuring water and provisions,

they

p. 11

they set out toward a large village scarcely two miles distant from there, fighting continually with a throng of Indians who with arrows, Bk. VIII. darts, and other weapons of theirs disputed their passage, and overcoming the stockades which they had built for their defense. The Spaniards having at last made themselves masters of the village, they sallied out from it frequently to make raids in the neighboring places, in which they had some very perilous skirmishes, until it came to a pitched and decisive battle on March 25. The battle was fought on the plains of Centla, a village not far from the other already mentioned. The army of the people of Tabasco was much superior in number; but in spite of their multitude they were utterly defeated, thanks to the better discipline of the Spaniards, the superiority of their arms, and the terror spread among the Indians by the size and the fire of the horses. Eight hundred of the people of Tabasco were left dead on the field; of the Spaniards one or two were killed, and more than sixty wounded. This victory was the beginning of the Spaniards' good fortune, and in memory of it they afterward founded a small city there under the name of Our Lady of Victory, (c) which for a long time was the capital of that province. They tried to justify their hostilities by the repeated protests which, before coming to blows, they had made to the people of Tabasco, that they had not come into that country as enemies to do any harm, but only as voyagers in need of obtaining, by the barter of their wares, what they required to continue their voyage, to which protests the Indians replied with a storm of arrows and darts.

Cortés took solemn possession of that country in the name of his sovereign with a strange ceremony, but one consonant with the customs and the chivalric ideas of that age. He took up his buckler, and drew his sword, with which he gave three blows on

To strengthen still further the dominion of his king, he summoned

the lords of that province, and persuaded them to render him obedi‐

ence, and to recognize him as their lawful lord; and, to give a more

advantageous idea of the king's power, he had a cannon fired before

them, and by a certain trick led them to believe that the neighing of

the horses was a show of their anger against the enemies of the

Spaniards. All appeared to assent to the proposals of the victor, and

listened with wonder and with satisfaction to the first truths of the

Christian religion, which Father Bartolomé de Olmedo, a learned and

exemplary religious of the Order of Our Lady of Mercy and chaplain

of the army, expounded to them through the interpreter Aguilar.

They then presented to Cortés, as a token of their submission, a few

small objects of gold, several garments of coarse cloth (for no others

were used in that province), and twenty female slaves, who were dis‐

tributed among the officers of the army.

Among these there was a young girl, noble, beautiful, of good inmous Indian woman telligence and of great spirit, born at Painala, a village of the Mexican

Doña Marina.

province of Coatzacoalco. (d) Her father had been a feudatory of the

crown of Mexico, and lord of several places. Her mother, having been

left a widow, married another nobleman, by

whom

(d) In a manuscript history that was in the library of the College of St. Peter and

St. Paul of the Jesuits of Mexico, one read that Doña Marina was born in Huilotla, a

village of Coatzacoalco. Gómara, followed by Herrera and Torquemada, says that she

was a native of Jalisco, and was thence taken by certain merchants of Xicallanco and

carried to their country; but this is certainly false; for Jalisco is distant from

Xicallanco more than nine hundred miles, and it is not known, nor is it even likely,

that there was ever any commerce between those two provinces, so widely separated

mother and the brother of Doña Marina, confirms the truth of our account, and says

that it is certain, and that he heard it from Doña Marina herself. To this is added the

tradition which has been preserved to this day in Coatzacoalco in conformity with

p. 13

whom she had a son. The love which they bore this fruit of their marriage drove them to the wicked plan of giving out that the firstborn Bk. VIII. was dead, so that the whole inheritance might go to him. Now to make it believed, they secretly handed her over to certain merchants of Xicallanco, a city situated on the borders of Tabasco, at a time when the daughter of one of their slave women had died, at whose death they made the same mourning that they would have made had their own daughter really died. The merchants of Xicallanco gave her, or rather sold her, to the people of Tabasco, their neighbors, and these finally presented her to Cortés, who was very far from thinking that that rare young woman was to serve with her tongue in the conquest of all that land. Besides the Mexican language proper to her own country, she knew well the Maya that was spoken in Yucatán and in Tabasco, and in a short time she learned Spanish too. Promptly instructed in the dogmas of the Christian religion, she was solemnly baptized, together with the other slave women, with the name of Marina. (*) She was constantly faithful to the Spaniards, and the services rendered by her cannot be sufficiently esteemed; for not only was she the instrument of their negotiations with the Mexicans, with the Tlaxcalans, and with the other nations of Anahuac; but she often saved their lives, warning them of the dangers that threatened them, and pointing out the way to avoid them. She accompanied Cortés on all his expeditions, serving him always as interpreter, often as counselor, and at times, to her misfortune, as his mistress. The son whom that Conqueror had by her was named Don Martín Cortés, Knight of the military order of Santiago, who on rash suspicions of rebellion was put to the torture in Mexico in 1568, those iniquitous and barbarous judges disregarding the incomparable services rendered by the parents of that illustrious accused to the Catholic King and to the whole nation

of

(*) The Mexicans, adapting the name Marina to their language, called her Malintzin, whence came the name Malinche, by which she is known among the Spaniards of Mexico.

p. 14

of Spain. (e) After the conquest she married a distinguished Spaniard Bk. VIII. named Juan Jaramillo. In the long and difficult journey which she

made in company with Cortés to the province of Honduras in 1524,

she had occasion, in passing through her native land, to see her moth‐

er and her brother, who presented themselves to her bathed in tears

and dismayed, since they feared that, she now finding herself in such

great prosperity under the protection of the Spaniards, she might

wish to avenge the wrong done her in her childhood; but she received

them, and comforted them with the utmost kindness, showing thus

that her piety and her generosity were in no way inferior to the other

gifts with which she had been adorned by Heaven. It did not seem to

us right to omit these particulars concerning a woman who was the

first Christian woman of the Mexican empire, who cuts so brilliant a

figure in the history of the Conquest, and whose name was, and is at

present, so celebrated, no less among the Mexicans than among the

Spaniards.

Cortés, having now made sure of the tranquillity of Tabasco, and

realizing that this was not a country from which gold could be got,

resolved to continue his voyage in search of another, richer one; but

as Palm Sunday was approaching, he wished first to give the people of

Tabasco some idea of the holiness of the Christian religion. On that

day holy Mass was celebrated with the greatest pomp possible, the

branches were blessed, and a solemn procession was held with mili‐

tary music, at which those Gentiles were present, amazed and edified,

there remaining from that hour in their hearts that seed of grace

which was to sprout and bear fruit in another, more fitting season.

When that ceremony was over, and he had taken leave of the lords

of

(e) Those who put Don Martín Cortés to the torture, and imprisoned the Marquis

The chief of them, named Muñoz, committed such outrages that the king, moved by

the complaints of the Mexicans, recalled him to court and gave him so severe and

strong a reprimand that on the following day he was found dead of vexation in his

chair.

of Tabasco, the fleet set out again on its voyage, and, proceeding westward after coasting the province of Coatzacoalco and crossing Bk. VIII. the mouth of the river Papaloapan, entered the port of San Juan de Ulúa on Holy Thursday, April 21. They had scarcely cast anchor when they saw coming from the coast of Chalchiuhcuecan toward the flagship two large canoes, in which were many Mexicans sent by the governor of that coast to learn who those were that had landed there in that new fleet, and what they wanted, and to offer them all the aid they might need for the continuation of their voyage: which made plain the vigilance of that governor and the hospitality of that nation. Having come aboard the flagship, and presented themselves to Cortés with very civil manners, they set forth their commission to him by means of Doña Marina and Aguilar; for, since Aguilar did not then understand Mexican, nor she Spanish, it was necessary in those first conferences with the Mexicans to employ three languages and two interpreters: Doña Marina explained to Aguilar in the Maya language what the Mexicans said in their own tongue, and Aguilar declared it in Spanish to Cortés. This general received the Mexicans courteously, and knowing how pleasing the trinkets of Europe had been to them the year before, he replied that he had come to that country for no other reason than to trade with them and to treat with their king about affairs of the highest moment; and, to put them the more under obligation, he made them taste the wine of Spain, and gave them some little gifts which he thought might be agreeable to them. (f) Torquemada says that Moctezuma, having been informed of the new fleet, already observed by the sentinels posted on the mountains, at once dispatched his ambassadors to pay reverence to the supposed god Quetzalcoatl, who, going with the utmost speed to the port of Chalchiuhcuecan, passed immediately on board the flagship on the very day on which the Spaniards landed there: that Cortés, warned of the error, and wishing to take advantage of it, received them seated on a high throne which he had promptly had made, where he let himself be worshiped, be dressed in the priestly habit of Quetzalcoatl, and have his neck adorned with a necklace of gems, and his head with a helmet of gold

On the first day of Easter, after the Spaniards had set foot on land, Bk. VIII.

and landed their horses and their artillery, and had made, with the

great train of servants; and the due ceremonies of courtesy and re‐

discussion Cortés wished, no less to bring good fortune to his enter‐

prise than to give those idolaters some idea of our religion, that the

holy sacrifice of the Mass should be celebrated in their presence. It

was sung also with the greatest solemnity possible, and this was the

first that was celebrated in the dominions of the Mexicans. (h)

set with gems, etc.; but this account is certainly false. The fleet left the river of

Tabasco on Holy Monday, and reached the port of Ulúa on Thursday. The mountains

of Tochtlan and of Mictlan, from which the fleet could most readily be observed, are

no less than 300 miles distant from the capital, nor is the latter distant from the port

of Ulúa by less than 220: so that even if the fleet had been seen on the very day on

which it sailed from Tabasco, it was impossible for the ambassadors to arrive at Ulúa

on Thursday. Besides, no record of such an event is to be found in any other author;

indeed from the account of Bernal Díaz it is seen that all this is false, and that the

Mexicans had already perceived the error into which the first fleet that appeared

there had led them.

seeking to win their goodwill by his attentions. As soon as they rose from the table, he led them aside to set forth his claims. He told Bk. VIII. them that he was a subject of Don Carlos of Austria, the greatest king of the East, whose goodness, greatness, and power he extolled in the most magnificent terms, and he added that this great monarch, having knowledge of that land and of the lord who reigned in it, had sent him to pay him a visit in his name, and to communicate to him by word of mouth some matters of great moment, and that he would therefore be glad to know where it would please him to hear the embassy. “Scarcely,” replied Teuhtlile, “have you arrived in this country, and you wish at once to see our king? I have listened with pleasure to what you have told me concerning the greatness and the goodness of your sovereign, but know that ours is no less good, nor a lesser king; indeed I marvel that there can be in the world another more powerful than he; but since you affirm it, I shall make it known to my sovereign, of whose goodness I trust that he will not only take pleasure in hearing such news of that great prince, but will also honor his ambassador. Accept in the meantime this gift, which in his name I present to you,” and, taking from a petlacalli, or chest woven of cane, several excellent pieces of gold work, he presented them to him, together with various curious pieces of featherwork, ten loads of fine cotton garments, and a great quantity of provisions. (i)

Cortés accepted the gift with a singular show of gratitude, and returned it with things of little value, but prized by them either because they were entirely new in that country or for their apparent brilliance. Cortés, perceiving their intention, ordered, to furnish the painters with a new subject capable of making a stronger impression on the king's mind, that his cavalry should gallop along the beach and perform some military evolutions, and that all the artillery should be fired at once: which was observed with such amazement as may be imagined by the two governors and by their numerous retinue, which, if we believe Gómara, was not less than four thousand men. Among the arms of the Spaniards Teuhtlile noticed a gilded helmet, which, because it was very like another that one of the principal idols of Mexico had, he asked of Cortés in order to show it to the king; and he granted it on the condition of having it back full of gold dust, under the pretext that he wished to see whether the gold that was taken from the mines of Mexico was like that of his own country. (k)

As soon as the paintings were finished, Teuhtlile took affectionate leave of Cortés, offering to return to him within a few days with the answer of his sovereign; and, leaving Cuitlalpitoc in his place, so that he might provide the Spaniards with everything they needed, he went to Cuetlachtlan, the place of his ordinary residence; whence he carried in person to the court the report, the paintings, and the gift of the Spanish general, as Bernal Díaz and Torquemada affirm, or else sent it all, as Solís conjectures, by the posts, which were always ready on the main roads to make a journey.

no less amazed than pleased at so great a wealth. “This gift,” added the ambassador, speaking to Cortés, “my sovereign sends for you and Bk. VIII. for your companions, since for your king he will send you shortly certain gems of inestimable value. In the meantime you may stay as long as you please on this beach to rest from the fatigues of so long a voyage, and to provide yourselves with whatever you need to return to your country. If you wish anything else of this land for your sovereign, it will be given you at once; but as regards your claim to go to the court, I am charged to dissuade you from so difficult and dangerous a journey, since you would have to travel through uninhabitable deserts and through enemy countries.” Cortés received the gift with the greatest expressions of his gratitude toward the royal beneficence, and responded as he could; but, without abandoning his request, he begged the ambassador to represent to the king the dangers and hardships they had suffered in so long a voyage, and the displeasure his sovereign would feel at seeing himself disappointed in his hope: that for the rest the Spaniards were of such a condition that neither hardships nor perils were capable of diverting them from their undertakings. The ambassador offered to make this known to the king, and courteously took leave together with Teuhtlile, Cuitlalpitoc remaining with a great number of people in a hamlet which they had formed of huts, not far from the camp of the Spaniards.

Cortés saw well, amid all the prosperity he had so far had, that he could not remain in that place; for besides the discomfort of the heat, and the nuisance of the mosquitoes, which abound too much along all that beach, he feared some damage to his ships from the violence of the north wind, to which that poor harbor is very much exposed: so he dispatched two ships under the command of Captain Montejo, that, proceeding along the coast toward the Pánuco, they might look for another, safer port. § 8. Meanwhile Teuhtlile returned to the camp of the Spaniards, and, Moctezuma’s gift for the Catholic King. drawing Cortés aside with the interpreters, told him that his lord

Moctezuma had been pleased with the new gift sent him by Cortés:

that what he was then sending him was destined for the great King of

Spain: that he wished him the utmost happiness; but, for the rest,

that he should send him no more messages, nor speak any more of

going to the court. The gift for the Catholic King consisted of several

works of gold, worth fifteen hundred sequins, of ten bales of most cu‐

rious featherwork goods, and of four gems so prized by the Mexicans

that, according to what Teuhtlile himself affirmed, each was worth

four loads of gold. That ill-advised king thought by his liberality to

oblige the Spaniards to abandon that country, not perceiving that the

love of gold is a fire which is the more inflamed the more copious the

fuel that is offered to it. Cortés was greatly vexed at the king's refusal;

but he was not on that account turned from his purpose, since the

natural constancy of his mind was spurred on still further by the al‐

lurement of wealth.

Before taking his leave, Teuhtlile observed that the Spaniards, on

hearing the strokes of the bell at the Ave Maria, knelt down before a

holy cross, and, full of wonder, asked why they adored that piece of

wood. Father Olmedo thereupon took occasion to expound to him

the principal articles of the Christian religion, and to reproach him

for the abominable worship of idols and the inhumanity of their sac‐

rifices; but such a discourse was altogether fruitless, since for those

peoples the time of sanctification had not yet come.

The next day the Spaniards found themselves so abandoned by the

Mexicans that not even one was to be seen along all that beach: an ef‐

fect, beyond doubt, of the order already given by the king to with‐

draw from the camp of those strangers both the people assigned to

their service and the provisions, if they persisted

in

p. 23

in their rash resolution. Such a piece of news caused great dismay among the Spaniards, because at every moment they feared that the Bk. VIII. whole power of that vast empire would fall upon their wretched camp: so Cortés had their provisions secured on the ships, and had his troops put under arms for defense. There is no doubt that both on this occasion and on many others that we shall recall in the History, Moctezuma could easily have destroyed utterly those few strangers who were to do him so much harm; but God preserved them, that they might be instruments of his justice, making use of their arms to punish the superstition, the cruelty, and the other crimes with which those nations had provoked his wrath. We do not however wish to justify the intention and the conduct of the Conquerors; but neither can we fail to recognize in the course of the conquest, in spite of incredulity, the hand of God, who was preparing that empire for its ruin, and was availing himself of the very misdeeds of men for the lofty designs of his Providence.

On this very day of such dismay for the Spaniards, they also had a Embassy of the lord token of Divine Providence. Two soldiers who were standing guard of Cempoala, and its

consequences. outside the camp saw coming toward them five men somewhat different from the Mexicans in their dress and in their ornaments, who, being led to the Spanish general, said in Mexican (for there was no one there who understood their own language) that they were of the Totonac nation, and sent by the lord of Cempoala, a city twenty-four miles distant from that place, to salute them, to learn who they were and whence they came, and to beg them to betake themselves to that city, where they would be well received, adding that they had not come sooner to the camp for fear of the Mexicans. The lord of Cempoala was one of those feudatories who lived restive under the yoke of the Mexicans; aware of the victory the Spaniards had gained in Tabasco and of their arrival at that port, this seemed to him the finest occasion to shake off the yoke with the help of such valiant men. Cortés

as

p. 24

as one who desired nothing more than such alliances to increase his Bk. VIII.

forces, after being sufficiently informed of the state and condition of

the Totonacs, and of the wrongs they suffered from the overbearing

power of the Mexicans, replied, thanking the Cempoalan for his

courtesy, and promising to go without delay to find him.

Indeed he at once announced his departure for Cempoala; but first

it was necessary for him to overcome the obstacles put in his way by

his own soldiers. Some partisans of the governor of Cuba, already

weary of the hardships they had endured, frightened by the perils

they foresaw, and longing for rest and for the comfort of their houses,

strongly urged the general to return to Cuba, exaggerating the scarci‐

ty of provisions and the rashness of so great an undertaking as that of

opposing so small a number of soldiers to the great power of the king

of Mexico, especially after having lost in those sands thirty-five men,

partly by the wounds received in the battle of Tabasco and partly on

account of the unwholesome air of that beach. Cortés, now with

gifts, now with promises, now with a little rigor opportunely applied,

now with other means devised by his rare intelligence, managed their

minds so well that he not only quieted the malcontents and induced

them to remain willingly in that rich country; but, advancing also in

his negotiations, he obtained that the army, in the name of the king,

and without any dependence on the governor of Cuba, should con‐

firm him in the supreme command, both political and military, and

that for the expenses already incurred by him on the expedition, and

to be incurred in the future, there should be assigned to him from

then on the fifth part of the gold that should be acquired, after de‐

ducting first what belonged to their king. He also had created the

magistrates and all the offices necessary for a colony, which he wished

to found on that coast.

Having therefore overcome these difficulties, and taken the mea‐

sures suited to the execution of his vast projects, he set out on the

march with his troops. His intention was not merely to refresh his

men, much distressed on that sandy

beach

p. 25

beach, and to seek allies, but also to choose a good site for the found‐

Bk. VIII. ing of the colony, since Cempoala lay on the road to Chiahuitztla, (*) in whose district was the new port already found by Captain Montejo. The army, with a part of the artillery, marched toward Cempoala in good order and prepared to defend itself should it be attacked, whether by the Totonacs, of whose good faith they were not yet sure, or by the Mexicans, whom they supposed offended by their resolution: precautions deemed superfluous by no good general, which were never neglected by Cortés even in the time of his greatest prosperity, being always useful for maintaining military discipline, and for the most part necessary to one's own safety. The ships proceeded along the coast to the port of Chiahuitztla.

Three miles before arriving at Cempoala twenty distinguished Cempoalans came to meet the army, and presented to Cortés a refreshment of pineapples and other fruits of the country, saluted him in the name of their lord, and begged his pardon that he had not come himself in person because he was prevented. They entered the city drawn up in battle order, fearing some treachery on the part of the inhabitants. A light horseman, having advanced as far as the principal square, and having seen a bastion of the palace of that lord, which, because it was freshly whitewashed and well polished, shone in the sun, thought it silver, and returned at full gallop to give so fine a piece of news to the general. Such deceptions are too frequent in those whose minds are filled by some strong passion. The Spaniards walked through the streets no less delighted than amazed at seeing that city, the largest they had seen until then in the New World, such a crowd of people, and such beautiful orchards and gardens. When they reached the great temple, the lord of that state came

to receive them at the door of the court; and although he was unable

to move on account of his immoderate fatness, he was a shrewd man

and of good understanding. After saluting them according to the cus‐

tom of the country, and incensing the general, he took leave, promis‐

ing to return as soon as they should have rested from the fatigues of

the journey. He lodged the whole Spanish troop in certain large and

handsome buildings that were within the enclosure of the temple,

whether built expressly for the lodging of strangers, like the one in

the great temple of Mexico, or intended as dwellings for the ministers

of the idols. There they were well treated, and supplied with whatever

they needed at the expense of that lord, who returned to them after

dinner in a portable chair, or litter, accompanied by a great number of

nobles. In the secret conference which he had with Cortés, this gen‐

eral extolled, by means of his interpreters, the greatness and power of

his sovereign, by whom he had been sent to that country and charged

with several commissions of the highest moment, and among others

with that of giving aid to oppressed innocence. “If, however,” he

added, “I can serve you in any way with my person and with my

troops, tell me so, for I will do it gladly.” On hearing these offers, very

skillfully introduced into the conversation, the lord of Cempoala

heaved a great sigh, which was followed by bitter complaints of the

misfortune of his nation. He told him that the Totonacs, having been

from time immemorial free, and ruled by lords of their own nation,

During this visit the lord of Cempoala made him a gift of gold work, which amounted, according to what some historians say, to about a thousand sequins.

The next day four hundred porters presented themselves to Cortés, sent him by that lord for the transport of the baggage, and then he learned from Doña Marina the custom that prevailed among those nations of supplying spontaneously, and without any payment, porters to every person of distinction who passed through their cities. § 10.

Here Cortés had another conference with the lord of that city and with the lord of Cempoala, who for this reason had had himself carried there. At the very time when they were discussing the matter of liberty, there arrived with a great retinue five Mexican nobles, collectors of the royal tributes, showing themselves exceedingly indignant against the Totonacs because they had dared to receive those strangers without awaiting the royal consent, and demanding of them twenty human victims to be sacrificed to their gods for the expiation of so great a crime. The whole city was troubled at such news, and especially the two lords, who felt themselves more

guilty. Cortés, having learned from Doña Marina the cause of their Bk. VIII.

therefore shut up in their cages, they who had entered that city with

such pride, and with such contempt for the Spaniards that they had

not even deigned to look at them as they passed before them.

No sooner had the Totonacs taken this first step than, gaining

courage from it, they went so far as to want to sacrifice them that

same night; but they were dissuaded by Cortés, who, having by that

capture won the love and respect of the Totonacs, wished also to gain

the goodwill of the Mexicans by freeing the prisoners. His artful and

double conduct certainly shows his talent; but it can be praised only

by those courtiers who know no other politics than the art of deceiv‐

ing men, and who, setting honesty at naught, seek only advantage in

their actions. Cortés therefore gave orders to his guards to take two

of the Mexicans out of the cages during the night, and to bring them

secretly into his presence, without the Totonacs being able to notice

it. So it was done, and the Mexicans were so obliged to the Spanish

general that they thanked him a thousand times, and advised him not

to trust the barbarous and perfidious Totonacs. Cortés charged them

to set before their sovereign his great displeasure at the attempt of

those mountaineers against his ministers; but that as he had set those

two at liberty, so he would do with the other three. They departed

immediately for the court, conducted by some Spaniards in a boat as

far as the borders of that province, and the next day Cortés pretend‐

ed to be angry with the guards, through whose carelessness those

prisoners had escaped; and so that the same might not happen with

the others, he said that he wished to secure them in

another

p. 29

another, closer prison, and to make this believed, he had them taken in chains onto the ships; but a little later he set them secretly at liber‐ Bk. VIII. ty, like the first.

He at once had word spread through all the mountains of the § 11. Totonacs that they were now free of the tribute which they paid to Confederation of the

Totonacs with the the king of Mexico, and that if by chance there were any other collec‐ Spaniards. tors of tribute, they should let him know at once so that he might seize them. At the sound of this news the sweet hope of liberty awoke throughout the whole nation, and several other lords began to come to Chiahuitztla, no less to thank their supposed liberator than to deliberate on the means of confirming their liberty. Some, who had not yet driven the fear of the Mexicans from their minds, held that pardon should be asked of the king for the attempt committed against his ministers; but through the suggestions of Cortés and of the lords of Cempoala and of Chiahuitztla the opposite view prevailed, to withdraw from the tyrannical domination of the Mexicans with the help of those brave strangers, offering to put a formidable army under the command of the Spanish general.

After Cortés had assured himself sufficiently of the sincerity of the Totonacs, and had informed himself of their forces, he took advantage of this favorable moment to induce that numerous nation to render obedience to the Catholic King. This act was celebrated with the intervention of the notary of the expedition, and with all the other legal solemnities.

This great affair having been happily concluded, Cortés took leave § 12. of those lords in order to carry out another project of the greatest im‐ Founding of portance, which he had formed a little earlier: namely, that of planti‐ Veracruz. ng on that coast a strong colony, which should serve them as a refuge in misfortune, a fortress to keep the Totonacs in the fealty they had already sworn to the Spaniards, a landing place for the new troops that might come to their aid from Spain or from the Antilles, and a warehouse in which to store the goods that might be sent to them by their countrymen, or that they themselves might wish to send to Europe. Thus was founded

refuse

(p) Almost all the historians have made a mistake concerning the founding of

Veracruz, for they say that the first colony of the Spaniards was the Antigua, or Old,

founded on the river of the same name, and they believe that there were no more

than two places called Veracruz, namely the old Veracruz and the new one planted on

the same sandy ground where Cortés landed; but there is no doubt that there have

been three cities of the same name of Veracruz. The first, founded in 1519 near the

port of Chiahuitztla, to which afterward only the name of Villa Rica remained; the

second, the Old Veracruz, founded in 1523 or 1524; and the third, the New Veracruz,

which today preserves the name of Veracruz, and was founded by order of the Count

of Monterrey, viceroy of Mexico, at the end of the sixteenth century or the beginning

of the seventeenth, and received from Philip III the title of city in 1615.

refuse the tribute which it ought to pay to its sovereign. They added that it was only out of regard for such guests that an army had not Bk. VIII. come to punish the rebellion of those peoples; but that in the end they would not remain unpunished. Cortés, after expressing his gratitude in the most fitting terms, sought to clear himself of the charge of friendship with the Totonacs by the necessity in which he had found himself of seeking provisions for his troops, because he had been abandoned by the Mexicans. He said then that, as to the tribute, it was not possible for that nation to serve two lords at once; that he hoped to betake himself shortly to the court, to satisfy the king more fully and to let him see the sincerity of his conduct.

The two princes, after watching with great pleasure and wonder the military exercise of the Spanish cavalry, returned to the court. The lord of Cempoala, who had been much displeased by that embassy, in order to bind the alliance with the Spaniards still closer, presented to Cortés eight maidens, well dressed, that they might marry the captains; and among them was a niece of his, whom he destined for the general himself. Cortés, who had spoken with him several times on the point of religion, told him that he could not accept them unless they first renounced idolatry and embraced Christianity; and he took occasion thereupon to set before him once more the pure and holy truths of our religion, and argued with all the force he could against the vain worship of their lying gods, and especially against the horrible cruelty of their sacrifices. To so fervent an exhortation the Cempoalan replied that, although he prized his friendship exceedingly, he could not oblige him by abandoning the worship of Smashing of the idols

of Cempoala. those gods, from whose hand they received health, abundance, and all the goods they had, and from whose anger, provoked by ingratitude, they must fear the severest punishments. Cortés’s martial zeal was inflamed the more by this reply; and turning to his soldiers he said to them, “Come, Spaniards, what are we waiting for? How can we endure that these men, who boast

of

p. 32

of being our friends, give to the statues and abominable images of the Bk. VIII.

Devil the worship that is due to our one and true God? How can we

permit them daily, before our very eyes, to sacrifice human victims to

those images so cruelly? Courage, soldiers, courage: now is the time

to show that we are Spaniards, and that we have received as an inheri‐

tance from our ancestors an ardent zeal for our holy religion. Let us

smash the idols, and remove from the sight of these infidels this

wicked fuel of their superstition. If we succeed, we shall do our God

the greatest service we can. If we die in the undertaking, He will re‐

pay us with eternal glory for the sacrifice we shall make to Him of our

life.”

The Cempoalan, who from the look of Cortés and the movements

of the soldiers saw clearly their intention, made a sign to his people

to prepare for the defense of their gods. The Spaniards were already

beginning to climb the steps of the temple when the Cempoalans,

stunned and indignant, cried out that they should beware of commit‐

ting that outrage, if they did not wish all the wrath of the gods to fall

at once upon them. Cortés, being incapable of being frightened by

their threats, answered them that he had already warned them many

times to give up that infamous superstition; that since they had never

been willing to take his counsel, so advantageous to them, neither did

he wish any longer to have them as friends; that if the Totonacs

themselves did not resolve to remove those abominable images, he

with his men would smash them; and that they should take good care

not to commit any hostility against the Spaniards, for they would at

once hurl themselves against them with such force that they would

not leave alive even one Totonac. To these threats Doña Marina

added another, more effective: that if they wished to oppose the pur‐

pose of those strangers, then instead of allying themselves with the

Totonacs against the Mexicans, they would ally themselves with the

Mexicans against the Totonacs, and then their ruin would be in‐

evitable. This argument turned the Cempoalan

from

p. 33

from the first impulse of his zeal, and the fear of the Mexican arms prevailing in his mind over that of his gods, he said to Cortés that he Bk. VIII. might do whatever he pleased; since they had not the heart to lay sacrilegious hands on their gods. No sooner had the Spaniards received this permission than fifty of their soldiers, climbing hastily to the top of the temple, took the idols from the altars and threw them down the steps. The Totonacs meanwhile, weeping bitterly and covering their eyes so as not to see so great a sacrilege, prayed with mournful voice to their gods not to punish in their nation the rashness of those strangers, since they could not prevent it without sacrificing themselves to the fury of the Mexicans. Nevertheless some, either less cowardly or more jealous of the honor of their gods, were preparing to take vengeance on the Spaniards, and would infallibly have come to blows if the Spaniards had not seized the lord of Cempoala and five principal priests, and, threatening to put them to death at once, had not compelled them to restrain the impetus of their fellow citizens.

After an action so bold, in which prudence had no part, Cortés commanded the priests to remove the fragments of the idols from his sight and to cast them into the fire. He was promptly obeyed; and, full of joy then, as if by smashing the idols he had wholly done away with idolatry and driven superstition from those peoples, he told the lord of Cempoala that he would now gladly accept the eight maidens whom he offered him; that from then on he would count the Totonacs among his friends and brothers, and would help them against their enemies in all their needs; that since they would no longer have to worship those detestable images of the Devil, their enemy, he wished to place in that same temple an image of the Mother of the true God, so that they might revere her and implore her protection in all their needs. Then he expatiated at length on the holiness of the Christian religion, and when he had finished he ordered the Cempoalan masons to remove from the walls of the temple those loathsome stains

History of Mexico, Vol. III. E of

p. 34

of human blood, which they kept there as trophies of their inhuman

Bk. VIII. religion, and to clean and whitewash the walls carefully. He had an al‐

tar made in the manner of the Christians, and placed on it the image

of Most Holy Mary. He committed this new sanctuary to the care of

four Cempoalan priests, on condition that they should always go

clean, and dressed in white in place of that black and melancholy

dress which they wore because of their ministry. And so that there

should never be a lack of lights before that sacred image, he taught

them the use of the wax which the bees made in their mountains; and

in order that in the time of his absence they should not put the idols

back there, or in some other way profane that sanctuary, he left there

one of his soldiers named Juan Torres, who on account of his age was

of little use for war, and who made to God the sacrifice of staying

among those infidels to promote His worship. The eight maidens, af‐

ter they had been sufficiently instructed, received holy baptism, tak‐

ing the name of Doña Catalina, the niece of the lord of Cempoala,

and that of Doña Francisca, the daughter of Cuexco, one of the princi‐

pal lords of that nation.

From Cempoala Cortés returned to the new colony of Veracruz,

where he had the consolation of reinforcing his small army with two

more captains and ten soldiers, who landed there from Cuba; to

whom were added shortly afterward six other men, who were taken

by a ruse from a ship of Jamaica.

Scarcely had the aforesaid procurators departed when Cortés, who § 16. was always turning great designs over in his mind, carried out an en‐ A famous deed of

Cortés. terprise which by itself would suffice to make known his magnanimity and to immortalize his name. To take from his soldiers every recourse, and with it every hope of returning to Cuba, and to reinforce his small army with all the sailors, after having punished with the extreme penalty two soldiers who were plotting treachery and flight in one of the ships, and with lesser corporal penalties three other accomplices, he induced by arguments and entreaties some of his confidants, and some of the pilots in whom he trusted most, to bore secretly one or two of the ships, to persuade everyone that they had sunk because they were worm-eaten by the broma, and to represent to him that the others were no longer fit for service for the same reason, having lain three months idle in the port. Cortés made use of such a trick lest his men conspire against him, finding themselves reduced to the hard necessity of conquering or dying. Everything was done as he had ordered, and with the consent of the whole army, after the sails, the rigging, the ironwork, and everything that could be of any use had been removed from the ships. “Thus by an effort of magnanimity,” says Robertson, “to which nothing is comparable in history, five hundred men agreed of their own will to shut themselves up in an enemy country, full of powerful and unknown nations, with every road to escape closed, remaining with the sole refuge of their valor and perseverance.” I do not doubt that if Cortés had not taken that counsel, the bold enterprise he was meditating would have been wholly impossible; since the soldiers

Bk. VIII. would have avoided the danger by flight, and the general himself

would have been forced to follow them.

He set out by way of Xalapa and Texotla, and after crossing with

great labor some deserted mountains, where the air was somewhat

raw, arrived at Xocotla (*), a considerable city with fine buildings,

among which rose thirteen temples and the palace of the lord, which

was built of stone and lime and furnished with a great number of

good halls and chambers, the most complete edifice that they had

seen until then in the New World. The king of Mexico had in that

place, and in the hamlets dependent on it, twenty thousand vassals

and five thousand Mexicans as a garrison. Olintetl (this was the name

of the lord of Xocotla) came to meet the Spaniards and lodged them

comfortably in his city; but in providing them with provisions he

showed himself at first somewhat sparing, until through

the

p. 37

the information supplied by the Totonacs, he formed a more favorable idea of their bravery, of the strength of their arms, and of their Bk. VIII. horses. In the conference which he had with the Spanish general, each boasted in rivalry of the greatness and power of their respective sovereigns. Cortés rashly demanded of him that he at once give obedience to the Catholic King and with some quantity of gold pay homage to his sovereignty. I have gold indeed, replied Olintetl, but I will not give it without the express order of my king. I shall soon see to it, said Cortés, that he orders you to give the gold, and all you have. If he commands me, rejoined Olintetl, not only the gold, and all my goods, but I shall give even my own person. But what Cortés could not obtain by his threats from that lord, he had by sheer liberality from two other persons of distinction of that valley, who, going to visit him at Xocotla, presented him with some gold necklaces and seven or eight slaves. Cortés found himself perplexed here about the road he ought to take to go to Mexico. The lord of Xocotla and the commanders of the Mexican garrison suggested to him that he should go by way of Cholula; but he judged more safe the advice which the Totonacs gave him, to set out by way of Tlaxcala; and in fact he would have perished in Cholula with his whole army had he gone there directly, as will be seen from what we shall say below. In order then to obtain from the Tlaxcalans permission to pass through their country, he sent to the senate four messengers from among those same Cempoalans whom he brought with him; but these, as we shall soon see, did not deliver the embassy in the name of the Spaniards, but in the name of the Totonacs, whether because the Spanish general so ordered, or because it seemed to themselves more fitting.

From Xocotla the Spanish army proceeded to Iztacmaxtitlan, whose population extended for ten or twelve miles in two unbroken rows of houses built on one and the other bank of a small river which runs through the middle of that long and narrow valley; but the city of Iztacmaxtitlan proper, furnished with good buildings and inhabited by six thousand souls

or

the Tlaxcalans, it was more exposed to their invasions. There the

Spaniards were very well received and given gifts.

They declare themselves subjects of a powerful monarch, in whose name they wish to visit you, offering to give you knowledge of the true God, and to help you against your ancient and mortal enemy. Our nation, by reason of that close friendship with your republic which it has constantly cultivated, advises you to receive these heroes as friends, who, although few, are worth many.” Maxixcatzin answered them in the name of the senate that they thanked the Totonac lords for the news and for the advice, and those brave strangers for the help which they offered to give them, but that to deliberate on a point of such great weight, time was needed; that in the meantime they should return to their lodging, where they would be treated with the distinction which their birth and their character demanded. The messengers withdrew, and the senate went into consultation.

Maxixcatzin, who was held in high esteem by all, both for his prudence and for his kindness, said that the advice given by such faithful friends, so hostile to the greatest enemy of the republic, was not to be rejected; that those strangers, judging by the tokens which the Cempoalans gave of them, seemed to be those heroes who, according to their tradition, were to arrive in that country; that the earthquakes which had been felt a short time before, the comet which was then letting itself be seen in the sky, and several other events of those last years were indications that the time for the fulfillment of that tradition had already arrived; that if they were immortal, the republic would try in vain to oppose their entry. “Our refusal,” he added, “might cause us the gravest harm, and for the king of Mexico it would be a subject of malicious pleasure to see introduced by force into Tlaxcala those whom the republic did not willingly

accept

p. 40

accept:” he was nevertheless of the opinion that they should be reBk. VIII.

ceived in a friendly manner. This view was heard with applause, but

Xicotencatl at once opposed it (s), an old man of great authority be‐

cause of his long experience in civil and military affairs. “Our law,” he

said, “orders us to welcome foreigners; not, however, enemies who

might do harm to the state. These men, who claim to enter our city,

seem to me more like monsters cast up by the sea, because it could

not bear them in its waters, than gods come from heaven, as some

foolishly imagine. Is it possible that they are gods, those who seek

with such avidity gold and pleasures? And what should we not fear

from them in a country so poor as ours, where we are deprived even

of salt? It does an injury to the bravery of our nation to think it so

easily conquered by a few strangers. If they are mortal, the arms of

the Tlaxcalans will make it plain to the world; and if they are immor‐

tal, there will be time to appease their anger with homage and to im‐

plore their clemency with repentance. Let their request, then, be re‐

jected, and if they should ever wish to enter by force, let their rash‐

ness be repulsed with arms.” This opposition of views between two

persons of such authority divided the minds of the other senators.

Those who were inclined toward trade and accustomed to a peaceful

life adhered to the opinion of Maxixcatzin, and the military men em‐

braced that of Xicotencatl. Temiloltecatl, one of the senators (t), sug‐

The senate accepted the counsel of Temiloltecatl; but before dispatching the messengers with the reply, it gave the proper orders to Xicotencatl. He was a fearless young man, an enemy of peace, and too much inclined to military glory; whence he welcomed the commission, as one that furnished him so fine an occasion to show his bravery.

Cortés, after waiting eight days for the reply of the senate, believing that such delay was the effect of that slowness which the majesty of potentates is wont to affect, and not doubting, because of what the Cempoalans told him, that he would be well received by the Tlaxcalans, left Iztacmaxtitlan with his whole army, which besides the Totonacs and the Spaniards was composed of a fair number of Mexican troops of the garrison of Xocotla, and marched in good order, as was his custom, as far as the great wall which on that side separated the states of Tlaxcala from those of Mexico; the description and measurements of which we gave already in the preceding book, where we discussed the fortifications of the Mexicans. It had been built by the Tlaxcalans to defend

On that day, which was August 31, some armed Indians let them‐

selves be seen, and as the cavalry, which was scouting the road,

wished to overtake them to get from them some information about

the resolution of the senate, two horses were killed and three others

wounded, and two men: a truly great loss for so small a cavalry. There

then appeared a body of troops estimated at about four thousand

men, against which the Spaniards and the allies hurled themselves,

and in a short time defeated it with the death of fifty Otomis. Soon

after there arrived two of the Cempoalan messengers with some

Tlaxcalans (y), who paid their compliments to Cortés in the name of

the senate and informed him of the permission which was granted

him to go with his army to Tlaxcala, blaming the Otomis for the hos‐

killed by them. Cortés pretended to believe it, and protested his grat‐

itude toward the senate. The Tlaxcalans took their leave, and with‐

drew their dead from the field to burn them. Cortés for his part had

the two horses that were killed buried, so that the sight of them

should not encourage the enemy to make new hostilities.

As for the account of the Cempoalan messengers who had remained in Tlaxcala, al‐

though it has been believed by almost all the Spanish historians, it is wholly incredi‐

ble for the reasons adduced by us. Dr. Robertson reports several conjectures to make

The next day the Spanish army marched as far as the neighborhood of two mountains, between which there were some ravines. There the Bk. VIII. other two Cempoalan messengers, who had remained in Tlaxcala, arrived, bathed in sweat and tears, and blaming the perfidy and cruelty of the Tlaxcalans; because, disregarding the law of nations, they had maltreated and imprisoned them, and had destined them for sacrifice, from which they said they had freed themselves by untying one another. This account of the Cempoalans was certainly false: for it was altogether impossible, not to say difficult, for the victims to free themselves by themselves, both because of the narrowness of the cage in which they were kept and because of the vigilance of the guards who watched them; besides which there is no record that the Tlaxcalans ever failed in the respect due to the character of ambassadors, and much less in the case of these, who belonged to a nation so closely joined to them in friendship as the Totonac. What seems more probable is that the senate, after sending back the first two messengers, kept the other two in order to dispatch them when they should have tested the strength of the Spaniards, and that they, impatient of waiting, absented themselves secretly, and sought to excuse their flight with such pretexts.

Scarcely had the Cempoalans finished their account when a

§ 19. Tlaxcalan squadron of about a thousand men appeared, who, as soon The war of Tlaxcala. as they came near the Spaniards, began to hurl stones, darts, and arrows at them. Cortés, after having declared to them before the royal notary of the expedition, by means of three prisoners, that they were not coming to do them any harm, and after having begged them not to treat them as enemies, seeing that this was of no avail, gave the order to repulse them. The Tlaxcalans went on retreating until they had led the Spaniards to the ravines, where the horses could not hold their own, and where a large army awaited them; as to the number of which there is a great variety among the authors. (z) There was a terrible fight there, in which the Spaniards believed

who had gone as messengers to Tlaxcala. They fought bravely for a good while

in view of both armies; but in the end the Cempoalan prevailed, who, having

thrown his opponent to the ground, cut off his head and carried it in triumph

to the camp. The victory was celebrated with acclamations and with military

music. The place where this battle was fought was called Teoatzinco, that is,

the place of the divine water.

That night the Spanish army encamped on a hill, where there was a tower,

about eighteen miles from the capital city of Tlaxcala. Huts were built there

for the convenience of the troops, and entrenchments were made for their

defense. There the camp of the Spaniards remained until the peace with the

Tlaxcalans.

Cortés, in order to oblige the Tlaxcalans by his hostilities to accept the

peace and friendship which he offered them, sallied forth on September 3

with his cavalry, a hundred Spanish foot, four hundred Cempoalans, and three

hundred Mexicans of the garrison of Iztacmaxtitlan, burned five or six neigh‐

boring hamlets, and took four hundred prisoners, whom, after caressing them

and giving them gifts, he set at liberty, charging the leading men

Bk. VIII. tion. These went straight to Xicotencatl the younger, who was encamped with a large army six miles from that hill. This proud Tlaxcalan replied that if the Spaniards wished to treat of peace they should go to the capital, where they would be victims consecrated to their gods, and their flesh destined to be food for the Tlaxcalans; that as for him, the next day he would come in person to give them a decisive reply. This resolution, made known to the Spaniards by the same messengers, threw them into such great consternation that that night they prepared for death by sacramental confession, without however neglecting the proper arrangements for their defense.

The next day, September 5, the Tlaxcalan army appeared, no less terrible to the sight by its innumerable multitude (A) than beautiful for the lovely variety of their plumes and other military ornaments. It was divided into five squadrons of ten thousand men each; each carried its own standard, and in the rear guard, according to the custom of that nation, came the common standard of the republic, which was, as we have said elsewhere, a golden eagle with outspread wings. The arrogant Xicotencatl, to show the little account he made of the Spaniards, and that he did not wish to take them by hunger but to conquer them by arms, sent them a refreshment of three hundred turkeys and two hundred baskets of tamalli, exhorting them to restore their strength for the battle. Shortly afterward he detached two thousand courageous men, so that they should

assault

(A) Cortés says that the army of the Tlaxcalans was of more than 149,000 men. Bernal Díaz affirms, as a thing certain and well known to him, that it consisted of only 50,000 men, namely 10,000 of Maxixcatzin, 10,000 of Xicotencatl, 10,000 of Tlehuexolotzin, 10,000 of Chichimecatecuhtli, one of the most distinguished lords of that republic, and 10,000 of Tecpanecatl, lord of Topoyanco, a considerable city of the same republic, although these names have been much altered by this author. assault the camp of the Spaniards. This assault was so violent that, Bk. VIII. forcing the entrenchments, they entered the camp and fought hand to hand with the Spaniards. The Tlaxcalans might have been victors this time, not so much because of the far superior number of their troops as because of their bravery and the quality of their arms, which were pikes, lances, swords, and darts with two and three points, had not the discord that arose among them made victory easier for their enemies. The son of Chichimecatecuhtli, who was commander of the body of troops of his father (*), having been insulted with words by the arrogant Xicotencatl, conceived such great indignation that he challenged him to a duel, which should decide the question of their courage and of their fortune; and not being able to obtain this satisfaction from him, in order to take some vengeance he withdrew from the field the troops who were under his orders, and induced the same course in those of Tlehuexolotzin. Despite so great a diminution of the army, the battle was very obstinate and bloody. The Spaniards, after having courageously repulsed the troops that had assaulted their camp, marched in battle order against the main body of the Tlaxcalan army. The slaughter which the artillery made in the crowded multitude was not enough to make the Tlaxcalans turn their backs, nor to prevent them from promptly filling those gaps which the dead left; indeed with their firmness and intrepidity they had thrown the Spaniards into confusion, in spite of the clamors and reproaches of Cortés and his captains. At last, after four hours of fighting, the Spaniards returned victorious to their camp, although the Tlaxcalans did not cease to assault them often on that same day. Of the Spaniards only one man was lost, and sixty were wounded, and all the horses. Of the Tlaxcalans very many were killed; but not even one corpse

(*) Solís says that Chichimecatecuhtli was an ally of the Tlaxcalans, but this is a manifest error, since we know from all the historians that he was a Tlaxcalan, and one of the most distinguished lords of that republic.
