Meanwhile the Mexicans continued their attacks with greater ar‐

dor. Cortés, although he made great slaughter of them, and had been

almost always victorious, nevertheless saw well that more was the

blood shed by his soldiers than the advantages arising from his victo‐

ries, and that in the end the want of provisions and munitions, and

the multitude of the enemy, must necessarily prevail over the bravery

of his troops and the superiority of his arms. Believing, therefore, the

prompt departure of the Spaniards to be absolutely necessary, he

called his captains to council to deliberate on the time and the man‐

ner of executing it. Their opinions were various. Some said that they

ought to depart by day, forcing their way with arms if the Mexicans

opposed them. Others wanted it done by night. This was precisely

the opinion of a soldier named Botello, who boasted of astrology, to

whom Cortés deferred more than was fitting, deceived by having seen

certain predictions of his accidentally fulfilled. It was resolved, then,

preferring the vain observations of that wretched soldier to the light

of military prudence, to go out by night with the greatest possible se‐

crecy, as if all their pains could suffice to conceal from the vigilance of

so great a number of enemies the march of nine thousand men with

their arms, their horses, their artillery, and their baggage. This was

fixed for the night of July 1 (G), a night ill-fated and memorable for

the Spaniards

forty men, to use for the passage of the ditches. He then had all the

gold, the silver, and the gems that they had until then amassed

brought out; he took from it the fifth part, which belonged to their

king, and delivered it to the officers of His Majesty, protesting the im‐

possibility in which he found himself of keeping and saving it. He left

the rest to his officers and soldiers, that each might take what he

wished; but at the same time he warned them how much better it

would be to abandon everything to the enemy; for, free of that

weight, they could save their lives with less difficulty. Many of them,

not wishing to remain deprived of the principal object of their de‐

sires, and of the sole fruit of their labors, loaded themselves with that

precious burden, under whose weight they perished, victims no less

of their own avarice than of the vengeance of their enemies.

p. 137

part of them passed the first ditch, or canal, happily, with the help of the bridge that they carried with them, without meeting any resis‐ Bk. IX. tance but the little that the sentinels, by whom that post was guarded, were able to make; but the priests, who were keeping watch in the temples, having become aware of it, cried to arms, and with their horns roused the people. In a moment the Spaniards found themselves assailed both by water and by land by an infinite number of enemies, who with their very multitude and disorder hindered one another in the attack. The fight at the second ditch was very terrible and bloody, the danger extreme, and extraordinary the efforts of the Spaniards to escape. The darkness of the night, the din of arms, the threatening cries of the combatants, the lamentable wailings of the prisoners, and the languid sighs of the dying formed a whole no less pitiable than horrible. Here was heard the voice of a soldier who called on his comrades for help, and there that of another who asked God for mercy. All was confusion, outcries, wounds, and slaughter. Cortés, like a good general, ran intrepidly hither and thither, passing and repassing the ditches often by swimming, encouraging some, helping others, and giving to the remnants of his scattered army all the order that could be given, not without great risk of being killed or taken prisoner. The second ditch was filled to such a degree with bodies that those of the rear guard passed over them. Alvarado, who commanded it, found himself at the third ditch so furiously pressed by the enemy that, being unable to face them, or to pass by swimming without evident danger of perishing at their hands, he planted his lance in the bottom of the ditch, and clasped its foot with his arms, and giving an extraordinary impulse to his body, leaped with one bound to the other side of the ditch. This leap, esteemed by all a prodigy of agility, gave to that place the name which it still keeps, of the Leap of Alvarado. (I)

Cortés, in spite of the greatness of his heart, could not restrain his tears at the sight of such a calamity. He sat down upon a stone in Popotla, a village near Tlacopan, not to rest from his labors, but to weep for the loss of his friends and companions. Amid so many disasters he had at least the comfort of hearing that his bravest captains, Sandoval, Alvarado, Olid, Ordaz, Ávila, and Lugo, his interpreters Aguilar and Doña Marina, and his engineer Martín López were safe, in whom he chiefly trusted to be able to repair his honor and conquer Mexico.

The Spaniards found themselves in so sorry a state, weakened by fatigue and wounds, that if the Mexicans had pursued them, not one would have been left alive; but hardly had they arrived at the last § 21. ditch that was on that causeway on the lake, when they returned to The toilsome march

of the Spaniards. the city, either because they were content with the slaughter already made, or because, having found the bodies of the king of Acolhuacan, of the royal princes of Mexico, and of other lords, they occupied themselves in mourning their death and in performing their obsequies. The same without doubt they will have done with their relatives and friends who were dead, leaving that day the streets and the ditches clean, and burning the bodies before they should infect the air with their corruption.

At daybreak the Spaniards found themselves at Popotla, scattered, weary, and distressed, and Cortés having gathered and reordered them, they marched through the city of Tlacopan, incessantly harassed by some troops of that same city, and of Azcapotzalco as well, as far as Otoncalpolco, a temple situated on the top of a small mountain nine miles to the west of the capital, where at present is the

Bk. IX.

Remedios, or of Succor. Here they fortified themselves as far as their

means allowed, in order to defend themselves with less toil against

the enemy troops, who harassed them all day. At night they rested a

little, and had some refreshment furnished them by the Otomis of

two neighboring hamlets, who lived impatient under the yoke of the

Mexicans. From this place they began to make their way toward

Tlaxcala, their only refuge in so great a calamity, by Cuauhtitlan,

Citlaltepec, Xoloc, and Zacamolco, pressed along the whole road by

several flying bands of the enemy. At Zacamolco they found them‐

selves so famished, and reduced to such misery, that they ate for sup‐

per a horse killed that same day by the enemy, and the general himself

had his share. The Tlaxcalans threw themselves on the ground to eat

the grass that they found there, imploring the aid of their gods.

Otompan.

not far from the city of Otompan, a numerous and brilliant army, ei‐

and perhaps magnified by their fear. These men persuaded them‐

their lives. This general drew up his languishing troops, extending the

front of that wretched army, so that its flanks might remain in some

way covered by the small wings of the little cavalry that was left to

him, and with a countenance full of fire said to them: “We find our‐

selves in such straits that it is necessary to conquer or die. Take heart,

Castilians, and trust that He who has until now delivered us from so

many dangers will save us from this one also.” At last battle was

p. 141

was very bloody, and lasted more than four hours. Cortés, seeing his

Bk. IX. troops diminish and in great part lose courage, and his enemies come on more proud notwithstanding the damage they received from the Spanish arms, took a bold and most perilous resolution, by which he obtained the victory, and put in safety the remnants of his wretched army. There came to his mind what he had often heard said, namely, that the Mexicans fell into disorder and fled whenever they saw their general dead, or the standard lost. Cihuacatzin, general of that army, clad in a rich military dress with a fine plume on his head, and with a gilded shield on his arm, was carried in a litter on the shoulders of some soldiers. The standard, which according to their custom he carried, was a net of gold fixed on the point of a pike, which he had strongly tied on his back, and which rose about ten palms above his head. (*) Cortés observed it in the center of that great multitude of enemies, and, resolved to deal a decisive blow, commanded his brave captains Sandoval, Alvarado, Olid, and Ávila to keep behind him to guard his back, and with others who accompanied him he hurled himself at the point where the enterprise seemed to him less difficult, with such impetus that he overthrew some to the ground with his lance, and others with his stirrups. Thus he advanced through the enemy ranks until he reached the general, who was accompanied by some officers, and with a thrust of his lance stretched him on the ground. Juan de Salamanca, a brave soldier who accompanied Cortés, dismounting promptly from his horse, put an end to his life, and taking from him the plume, presented it at once to Cortés. (O) The enemy army, as soon as it saw its general dead and the standard taken, was thrown into confusion

and began to flee. The Spaniards, encouraged by the glorious deed of Bk. IX. their leader, pressed hard upon them, making a great slaughter of

them.

This victory was one of the most famous that the Spanish arms

had in the New World. The Spanish general distinguished himself in

it above all, of whom his captains and soldiers said afterwards that

they had never seen so great a courage and so great an activity as that

which he displayed that day; but he received a grave wound in the

head, which, growing worse every day, brought his life into extreme

danger. Bernal Díaz justly praises the bravery of Sandoval, and shows

how large a part this famous officer had in the victory, giving courage

to all no less by example than by exhortations. María de Estrada, wife

of a Spanish soldier, has likewise been celebrated by the Spanish his‐

torians; armed with lance and buckler she ran amid the enemy host

wounding and killing with an intrepidity most strange in her sex. Of

the Tlaxcalans Bernal Díaz says that they fought like lions, among

whom Calmecahua, captain of the troops of Maxixcatzin, distin‐

guished himself. He took at baptism the name of Don Antonio, and

became famous, even more than for his bravery, for his very long life

of a hundred and thirty years.

The loss of the enemy was undoubtedly great, but much less than

that which several authors report, who make it amount to twenty

thousand men: a number altogether incredible considering the

wretched state to which the Spaniards were reduced, and the lack of

artillery and other firearms. On the contrary, the loss of the Spaniards

was not so small as Solís represents it; (P) for there perished almost

The Spaniards, at length weary of pursuing the fugitives, resumed the road toward Tlaxcala by the eastern part of that plain, where they stayed that night in the open, and the general himself, after the fatigue and the wounds he had received that day, kept guard in person for their greater safety. The Spaniards were by now no more than four hundred and forty. Besides those who had been killed in the fights preceding the night of their ill-fated departure from Mexico, there perished in it and in the six following days eight hundred and seventy, as Bernal Díaz, an eyewitness, often affirms, many of whom, having been taken prisoner by the Mexicans, were inhumanly sacrificed in the great temple of the capital.

Quite another idea of the battle of Otompan is given us by those who were there, as can be seen in the letters of Cortés and in the True History of Bernal Díaz. “Oh, how furious and fearful to behold,” says this author, “was that battle! how they fought hand to hand, and with what fury the dogs (so he calls the enemy by way of insult!) attacked us! What wounds and slaughter they inflicted on us with their lances and swords, etc.,” and after a few lines he says thus: “I repeat that they wounded and killed many of our soldiers.”

their calamities by their dearest friends; but they were soon undeBk. IX.

ceived, seeing their sincere demonstrations of esteem and of compas‐

sion for the past misfortunes. As soon as the news of their arrival was

had, the four heads of that republic went to Hueyotlipan to compli‐

Huexotzinco and by many nobles. Prince Maxixcatzin, although af‐

flicted by the death of his dear daughter Doña Elvira, tried to console

Cortés with the hope of vengeance, assuring him of finding it in the

courage of the Spaniards and in the forces of the republic, which

from that time he promised him, and the other lords offered the

same. Cortés thanked them for their singular goodwill, and taking the

standard that had been taken the day before from the Mexican gener‐

al, he presented it to Maxixcatzin, and to the other lords he gave

some valuable spoils. The Tlaxcalan women implored Cortés to

avenge the death of their sons and relatives, and vented their grief in

a thousand imprecations against the perfidy of the Mexicans.

Having rested three days in this place, they passed to the capital of

the republic, fifteen miles distant from there, to tend their wounds,

of which eight soldiers died. The concourse of people at their entry

into Tlaxcala was equal to, and perhaps even greater than, that which

there was then, when they first entered it. The reception that

Maxixcatzin gave them, and the care that he took of them, were wor‐

thy of his generous spirit and of his sincere friendship. The Spaniards

recognized themselves every day more obligated to that nation,

whose friendship, constantly cultivated, was the most efficacious

means not only for the conquest of the capital of the Mexican em‐

pire, but also for that of all the provinces that opposed the progress

of the Spanish arms, and for the subjugation of the barbarian

Chichimecs and Otomis, by whom the Conquerors were so much

harassed.

While the Spaniards rested in Tlaxcala from their fatigues, and

tended their wounds, the Mexicans were exerting themselves

to

p. 145

to remedy the ills of the court and of the kingdom. The losses they

Bk. IX. had suffered during the past year were indeed great and lamentable; for besides the great sums of gold, silver, gems, and other precious things which they had spent, partly in gifts made to the Spaniards and § 24. partly in homage to the King of Spain, of which they doubtless recov‐ Election in Mexico,

and the measures of ered a certain amount, the reputation of their arms had been tar‐ King Cuitláhuac. nished, and respect for the Crown of Mexico diminished; the Totonacs and other peoples had withdrawn from their obedience; their enemies had grown more insolent; the temples were damaged and many houses of the capital ruined, and above all they lacked their king, many persons of the royal house, and a great part of the nobility. To these losses received from the Spaniards were added those which they brought upon themselves by the civil war, of which we owe our knowledge to the manuscripts of a Mexican historian who was at that time at that court and survived the ruin of the empire by some years.

While the Spaniards were in the court, distressed by hunger through the hostility of the Mexicans, several lords of the first nobility, either to favor the Spaniards’ party, or, what seems more probable, to succor their king, who, being among the Spaniards, must have suffered the same want, supplied them secretly with provisions, and perhaps even declared themselves openly in their favor, trusting in their own authority. For this reason so fatal a dissension broke out among the Mexicans that it could not be quenched except by the death of many illustrious persons, and particularly of Cihuacoatl, of Tzihuacpopoca, of Cipocatli, and of Tencuecuenotzin, some of them sons and others brothers of King Moctezuma, according to what the aforesaid historian affirms.

The Mexicans had need of setting at the head of their nation a man capable of restoring its honor and of repairing the losses suffered in the last year of Moctezuma’s reign. Prince Cuitláhuac was elected King of Mexico, either a little before or a little after the defeat of the Spaniards. He was, as we have already said, lord of Iztapalapa,

History of Mexico, Vol. III. T Privy

p. 146

privy councilor to King Moctezuma his brother, and Tlachcocalcatl, or Bk. IX.

general of the army. He was a wise man and of great talent, as his ene‐

my Cortés bears witness, liberal and magnificent like his brother. He

took great delight in architecture and in the cultivation of gardens, as

was seen in the magnificent palace which he built in Iztapalapa, and

in the celebrated garden which he planted there, both of which are

mentioned with great praise by nearly all the ancient historians. His

bravery and his military skill won him great esteem among his coun‐

trymen; some Spaniards well informed of his character affirmed that

if he had not been cut off by death, the capital would never have been

taken by the Spaniards. (Q) It is to be believed that the sacrifices

which were made at the festival of his coronation were of those

Spaniards whom he himself took prisoner on the night of their

defeat.

No sooner were the festivities of his coronation ended than he ap‐

plied himself to remedying the ills of the court and of the empire. He

gave orders to repair the damaged temples and to rebuild the ruined

houses, enlarged and improved the fortifications of the capital, sent

messengers to the provinces of the empire, encouraging them to the

common defense of the state against those foreign enemies, and

promised to relieve of every tribute those who should take up arms in

favor of the Crown. He sent also ambassadors to the republic of

(Q) Solís gives Cuitláhuac the name of Quetlabaca, and says of him that he lived on

the throne but a few days, but such as sufficed to leave among his countrymen the memory of his

name almost effaced by his worthlessness. But this is false, and contrary to the account of

Cortés, Bernal Díaz, Gómara, and Torquemada, authors far better informed than

when it remained indelible among the Spaniards, who regarded him as the author of

their terrible defeat of July 1, as they themselves testify? Cortés so remembered

Cuitláhuac, and kept such resentment against him for those disasters, that when he

found himself with forces sufficient to undertake the siege of Mexico, wishing to

avenge himself on that king, and unable to take vengeance on his person, since he

had already died, he took it on the city he had favored. This was the motive, as

Cortés himself says, of his expedition against Iztapalapa.

Tlaxcalans had until then been mortal enemies, it was now necessary for them to unite, as natives of one and the same country, as peoples of one language, and as worshipers of the same gods, against the common enemies of the fatherland and of religion; that they had already seen the bloody slaughter which those inhuman and haughty strangers had made in Mexico and in other places, their sacrilegious attempts against the sanctuaries and against the venerable images of the gods, their ingratitude and perfidy toward his brother and predecessor and toward the most respected personages of the Anahuatlacas, and their insatiable hunger for gold, which led them to violate the most holy laws of friendship; that if the republic continued to second the perverse designs of those monsters, it would receive from them in the end the same recompense that king Moctezuma received for the humanity with which he welcomed them to his court and for the liberality with which he favored them for so long; that the Tlaxcalans would be detested by all nations for having given aid to such wicked usurpers, and that the gods would hurl down upon the republic the whole fury of their wrath for its being confederated with the enemies of their worship. If, on the contrary, the Tlaxcalans declared themselves, as he begged them, enemies of those men hated by Heaven and by Earth, the court of Mexico would make a perpetual alliance with them, and would thereafter have free trade with the republic, whereby the latter could escape the misery to which it had until then been reduced; all the nations of Anahuac would be obliged to them for so important a service; and the gods, appeased by the blood of such victims, would send the rain needed in their fields, would make their arms fortunate, and would render the name of the Tlaxcalans famous throughout the earth.

The senate, after hearing the embassy and dismissing

T2 from

p. 148

from the audience hall the ambassadors, according to their custom, Bk. IX. remained there to deliberate on that great affair. There were not lack‐

ing some to whom the proposals of the court of Mexico seemed just

and conducive to the happiness of the republic, exaggerating the ad‐

vantages that were offered to them and, on the other side, the disas‐

trous outcome of the Spaniards’ enterprise in Mexico and the slaugh‐

ter made among the Tlaxcalan troops who had been there under their

orders. Among others the younger Xicotencatl raised his voice, who

had always been a mortal enemy of the Spaniards, and endeavored

with every argument he could to persuade them to the alliance with

the Mexicans, adding that it would be much better to preserve the

ancient customs of their nation than to submit to the new and extrav‐

agant usages of that untamed and imperious people; that he could not

imagine another occasion more opportune for freeing themselves en‐

tirely from the Spaniards than now, when they found themselves re‐

duced, weakened, and cast down. Maxixcatzin, who on the contrary

was sincerely attached to the Spaniards, and was endowed with a bet‐

ter mind for knowing the law of nations and a will better disposed to

observe it, rejected the opinion of Xicotencatl, condemning as an

abominable perfidy the counsel to sacrifice to the vengeance of the

Mexicans those men harassed by fortune, who had sought their asy‐

lum in Tlaxcala, trusting in the protestations and the demonstrations

of the senate and of the nation. He added that if they flattered them‐

selves with the advantages which the Mexicans offered, he hoped for

greater ones from the bravery of the Spaniards; that if there was rea‐

son not to trust the Spaniards, there was still less reason to trust the

Mexicans, of whose perfidy they had so many examples; finally, that

no other crime would be capable of so greatly provoking the wrath of

the gods and of obscuring the glory of the nation as such wickedness

against those innocent guests. Xicotencatl pressed his counsel by pre‐

senting to the senators a loathsome portrait of the character and cus‐

toms of the Spaniards. The altercation was so great, and so heated the

minds, that Maxixcatzin, carried away by anger, gave a push to

Xicotencatl

p. 149

Xicotencatl, and hurled him down some steps that were there, calling him

Bk. IX. seditious and a traitor to the fatherland. Such a demonstration, made by a man so circumspect and so respected and loved by the nation, obliged the senate to have Xicotencatl imprisoned.

The resolution taken there was to answer the embassy that the republic was ready to accept peace and friendship with the court of Mexico, provided it were not to be made by an action so unworthy and a crime so enormous as that of sacrificing its guests and friends; but when the ambassadors were sought in order to announce to them the senate’s answer, they had already secretly departed from Tlaxcala, because, having observed that rabble somewhat restless at their arrival, they feared that it might attempt something against the respect due to their character. It is to be believed, however, that the senate sent Tlaxcalan ambassadors to carry the answer to that court. The senators took pains to conceal from the Spaniards the motive of the embassy and all that had happened in the senate; but in spite of their diligence Cortés learned of it, and thanked Maxixcatzin, as was just, for his good offices, and offered to live up to the favorable idea he had of the bravery and friendship of the Spaniards.

Not content with such proofs of its great fidelity, the senate once more § 26.

Baptism of the four gave obedience to the Catholic King, and, what is more, the four heads of the lords of Tlaxcala. republic, moved by the grace of the Holy Spirit, renounced idolatry, and after being well instructed were baptized by Father Juan Díaz, chaplain of the Spanish army, with Cortés and his principal captains as their sponsors. This ceremony was celebrated with the greatest demonstrations of joy, both of the Spaniards and of the Tlaxcalans. Maxixcatzin was named at baptism Don Lorenzo, Xicotencatl the elder Don Vicente, Tlehuexolotzin Don Gonzalo, and Citlalpopoca Don Bartolomé. (R)

to be cut for the construction of thirteen brigantines; but while he

was forming these great projects, many of his soldiers were turning

over thoughts very different. They saw themselves diminished in

number, poor, in bad condition, and stripped of arms and horses.

They could not forget the terrible conflict and the tragic night of July

1, nor did they wish to expose themselves again to such misfortunes.

They fed one another’s ideas and fears, and blamed the obstinacy of

their general in so rash an enterprise. From private murmurings they

went on to make him a formal petition, wishing to oblige him with

The lords of the province of Tepeyacac, bordering on the republic § 28.

War of the Spaniards of Tlaxcala, had declared themselves friends of Cortés and subjects of against the people of

Tepeyacac. the King of Spain ever since that horrible butchery which the Spaniards made in Cholula; but seeing the Spaniards afterward cast down and the Mexicans victorious, they returned to the obedience of the King of Mexico, and to win his favor they killed some Spaniards who were traveling from Veracruz to Mexico, unaware of the tragedy of their companions; they admitted a Mexican garrison into their towns, and occupied the road that led from Veracruz to Tlaxcala, and, not content with this, made some raids into the lands of that republic. Cortés resolved to make war upon them, no less to punish their perfidy than to secure the road from that port for the reinforcements which he expected from there. He was urged to such an expedition also by the younger Xicotencatl, who had already been restored to liberty through the mediation of the same Spanish general, and who, to remove any suspicion that might exist against him on account of what had happened in the senate, offered to aid him in that war with a good army. Cortés accepted the offer; but before taking up arms, he amicably requested some satisfaction from the people of Tepeyacac, and exhorted them to leave the Mexican party, promising to pardon them

Their first expedition was against Zacatepec, a place belonging to the con‐

federation of the people of Tepeyacac. Its inhabitants laid an ambush for the

Spaniards. They fought on both sides with great courage and obstinacy; but

the people of Zacatepec were in the end defeated, very many of them remain‐

miles southeast of Tepeyacac, which the Spaniards entered in triumph after

winning a battle little less difficult than that of Zacatepec. From Acatzinco

Cortés sent several detachments to burn some places in those surroundings

and to bring others under his obedience, and when it seemed to him time to

The Mexican troops that were in the garrisons of that province § 29.

War of withdrew from it, because they had not forces sufficient to resist the Cuauhquechollan. power of their enemies; but at the same time there appeared above the city of Cuauhquechollan (**), more than forty miles from Tepeyacac toward the southwest, an army of Mexicans sent there by King Cuitláhuac to prevent the Spaniards from passing that way to the court, if ever they should attempt it. Cuauhquechollan was a considerable city, where there were some five to six thousand families, very pleasant, and strong no less by nature than by art. It was naturally defended on one side by a high and steep mountain, and on another by two rivers not far apart. The whole city was surrounded by a strong wall of stone and lime, about twenty feet high and twelve wide, with a good parapet all along it, which was almost three feet high. There were no more than four gates by which to enter, in those parts where the ends of the wall were doubled, forming two semicircles, as they are seen represented in the figure we gave in Book VII. The difficulty of the entrance was increased by the elevation of the ground of the city, which was as great as the height of the wall itself, so that to enter it was necessary to climb some quite high steps.

The lord of that city, who was partial to the Spaniards, sent an embassy to Cortés, protesting his vassalage to the King of Spain, who had already been acknowledged lord of that land in the celebrated assembly which King Moctezuma held with the Mexican nobility in the presence of Cortés: that he

the Spanish commander became suspicious that the Huexotzincas

had secretly confederated with the people of Cuauhquechollan and

with the Mexicans to ruin the Spaniards utterly. This suspicion,

caused by sinister information and made more probable by the multi‐

him to return to Cholula, where he had some of the more distin‐

guished Huexotzincas and the messengers of Cuauhquechollan

seized, and sent them under good guard to Cortés, that he might

make his inquiries concerning the alleged treachery.

Cortés was greatly vexed by such conduct toward friends so faith‐

ful as the Huexotzincas. Nevertheless he examined them diligently,

discovered the innocence and good faith of both parties, and per‐

ceived that the past disasters had made the Spaniards more fearful,

and that fear led them, as it usually does, to suspect more than was

fitting. He caressed and made gifts, as much as he could, to the

Huexotzincas and the people of Cuauhquechollan

and

p. 155

and, accompanied by them, he marched at once for Cholula with a

Bk. IX. hundred foot and ten horse, determined to carry out this enterprise in person. He found the Spaniards in Cholula frightened, gave them courage, and then marched for Cuauhquechollan with the whole army, which then consisted of more than three hundred Spaniards and more than a hundred thousand allies. Such was the readiness of those peoples to arm themselves against the Mexicans in order to escape from their domination. Before arriving at Cuauhquechollan Cortés was informed by that lord that all measures had already been taken: that the Mexicans trusted in their sentinels posted on the towers of the city and on the roads; but that these had already been secretly seized by the citizens.

No sooner did the people of Cuauhquechollan see the army that was coming to their relief than they assaulted with armed hand the lodgings of the Mexicans with such violence that before Cortés entered the city they presented to him forty prisoners. When that general entered, three thousand citizens were fighting at the principal lodging of the Mexican officers, who, although much inferior in number, defended themselves nonetheless with such courage that the people of Cuauhquechollan could not take the house, even though they had made themselves masters of the flat roofs. Cortés gave it the assault and took it; but in spite of the pains he took to capture someone from whom to learn the present state of the court, the Mexicans fought with such obstinacy that all were killed, and he could scarcely gain some light from a dying officer. The other Mexicans who were scattered about the city rushed out of it in order to join the main body of the army, encamped in a high place that commanded all the surroundings, which in a moment drew up in battle order and entered the city, setting fire to the houses. Cortés affirms that he had never seen another army of finer appearance, because of the gold and plumes with which it was seen adorned. The Spaniards ran to the defense with their cavalry and many thousands of allies, and forced them to retire to

§ 30. The army rested three days in Cuauhquechollan, and on the fourth War of Itzocan. marched toward Itzocan (*), a city of three to four thousand families,

situated at the foot of a mountain, about ten miles from

Cuauhquechollan, and surrounded by a deep river and by a small wall.

Its streets were well ordered, and its temples so many that, great and

small, they seemed to Cortés to number about a hundred; its climate

is hot because it is situated in a deep valley enclosed by high moun‐

tains, and its soil, like that of Cuauhquechollan, is most fertile, and

shaded by trees with beautiful flowers and excellent fruits. That state

was then ruled by a personage of the royal blood of Mexico, to whom

Moctezuma had given it in fief after having put to death, I know not

for what cause, the lawful lord who possessed it; and at present there

was a garrison there of five to six thousand men of Mexican troops.

Having learned all this from the lord of Cuauhquechollan, Cortés was

moved to the expedition against Itzocan. His army had so increased

that it amounted, by what he himself affirms, to about a hundred and

twenty thousand men. He assaulted the city on that side where the

entrance was less difficult. The people of Itzocan, seconded by the

royal troops, offered some resistance at first; but being in the end

overcome by the far superior forces of the assailants, they were

thrown into confusion, and fled by the opposite side of the city, and

Cortés, having returned to the city, had fire set to all the sanctuaries, and by means of some prisoners summoned the citizens, who were scattered about the mountains, giving them safe-conduct, that they might return without any fear to live in their houses.

The lord of Itzocan had absented himself from the city and set out on the road to the court as soon as the enemy army was seen. That sufficed for the nobility, to whom perhaps that lord was not acceptable, to declare the state vacant; whereupon they agreed, with the authority and protection of Cortés, to give it to a son of the lord of Cuauhquechollan and of a daughter of that lord whom Moctezuma put to death; and since he was still a boy of a few years, there were assigned to him as guardians his own father, an uncle of his, and two nobles. This boy was soon instructed in the Christian doctrine and baptized.

The fame of the victories of the Spaniards flew at once throughout the land, and drew several peoples to the obedience of the Catholic King. Besides Cuauhquechollan, Itzocan, and Ocopetlayocan, a large city not far from those two, (V) there came some lords to pay homage to the Crown of Castile from eight places of Coaixtlahuacan (*), part of the great province of Mixtecapan, more than a hundred and twenty miles to the south of Cuauhquechollan, all seeking eagerly the alliance and friendship of men so valiant.

The victories of the Spaniards and the multitude of their allies so § 32.

Havoc wrought by enlarged their name, and gained for Cortés such authority among the smallpox. Death those peoples, that he was the arbiter of their differences, and to him, of King Cuitláhuac

and of Prince as if he were the supreme lord of that land, they came to obtain the Maxixcatzin, and

election of King confirmation of the investiture of vacant states, as happened in those Cuauhtémoc. of Cholula and of Ocotelolco in Tlaxcala, both vacant through death caused by smallpox. This scourge of the human race, entirely unknown until then in the New World, was brought there by a Black slave of Narváez. This man gave it to the Cempoalans, and from them the contagion spread through the whole Mexican empire with unspeakable damage to those nations. Many thousands of men perished of it, and some places were left depopulated. Those whose constitution prevailed over the violence of the disease were left so disfigured, and marked with such deep traces of the poison on the face, that they filled with horror all who looked at them. Among the other evils caused by this new disease, there was all too keenly felt by the Mexicans the death of their king Cuitláhuac, after three or four months of reign, and by the Tlaxcalans and the Spaniards that of Prince Maxixcatzin.

The Mexicans elected as king Cuauhtémoc, nephew of the late Cuitláhuac, since there remained no brother of the last two kings. He was a young man of twenty-five years and of great spirit, and although by reason of his age not much practiced in the trade of war, he nonetheless continued the military measures of his predecessor. He married his cousin Tecuichpotzin,

daughter

daughter of King Moctezuma, and already the wife of his uncle

The death of Maxixcatzin was greatly mourned by Cortés, as much

for the friendship he had formed with him as also because to him was

chiefly due that harmony which existed between the Tlaxcalans and

the Spaniards. Accordingly, after having made the road to Veracruz

safe, and having sent to the court of Spain Captain Ordaz with a dis‐

tinct account, written to the Emperor Charles V, of all that had be‐

fallen him until then, and Captain Ávila to the island of Santo

Domingo to solicit new reinforcements for the conquest of Mexico,

he set out from Tepeyacac for Tlaxcala, and entered it dressed in

black, and making other demonstrations of grief for the death of the

prince his friend. At the request of the Tlaxcalans themselves, and in

the name of the Catholic King, he conferred the vacant state of

Ocotelolco, one of the four principal ones of that republic, upon a

son of the deceased prince, a boy of twelve years, who at baptism

took the name of Don Juan Maxixcatzin, (*) the name of the father

being from then on a surname of the son and of all his illustrious de‐

scent; and to do him some particular honor out of regard for the mer‐

its of his father, he dubbed him a knight in the manner of Castile.

§ 33. Elevation of Prince At that same time, though from a very different cause, occurred Coanacochtzin, and the death of Prince Cuicuitzcatzin, whom Moctezuma and Cortés

death of

Cuicuitzcatzin. had placed on the throne of Acolhuacan in place of his unfortunate

brother Cacamatzin. He was not permitted to enjoy his counterfeit

dignity, because soon the very man who had given him the crown

took away his liberty. He left Mexico among the other prisoners on

that night of the Spaniards' defeat; but he then had the fortune, or

rather misfortune, to escape, since he was soon to lose more

ignominiously

his

(*) Solís says that he was called Lorenzo; but that was the name of the father; the

son was called Juan, as Torquemada attests, who learned it from the Tlaxcalans

p. 161

his life. He accompanied the Spaniards in their conflicts as far as

Bk. IX. Tlaxcala, where he stayed until, either impatient of oppression or eager to recover the throne, he fled secretly to Texcoco. There reigned then at that court his brother Coanacochtzin, to whom, Cacamatzin being dead, the crown belonged according to the law of that kingdom. No sooner had Cuicuitzcatzin presented himself there than he was made prisoner by the royal ministers, who gave prompt notice of it to the king, then absent in Mexico. He let King Cuauhtémoc his cousin know of it, who, thinking that fugitive prince a spy of the Spaniards, was of opinion that he should be put to death. Coanacochtzin, either to please that monarch, or rather to remove from Cuicuitzcatzin the occasion of wishing to recover the throne to the prejudice of his own right and of the peace of the kingdom, had him put to death without delay. Thus ended that unfortunate prince, whose exaltation served for nothing but to make his fall greater and more resounding. (Z)

be employed in the siege of Mexico; and when it seemed to him that it was time to march, he reviewed his troops, which consisted of forty Bk. X. horse and five hundred and fifty foot. He divided that small cavalry into four parts, and the infantry into nine companies, armed some with muskets, others with crossbows, others with sword and buckler, and others with pikes. From his horse, on which he was drawing up his troops, he made them this address: “Friends and comrades of mine, any discourse that I might wish to make to animate your bravery is altogether superfluous, since we all acknowledge ourselves obliged to repair the honor of our arms and to avenge the death of our Spaniards and allies. Let us go to the conquest of Mexico, the most glorious enterprise of any that can ever be offered to us in all our life. Let us go to punish at one stroke the perfidy, the pride, and the cruelty of our enemies, to enlarge the dominions of our sovereign by adding to them a kingdom so great and so rich, to smooth the road for the Gospel, to open the gate of Heaven to so many millions of souls, to secure by the toil of a few days the comfort of our families, and to make our name immortal: incentives capable of encouraging even the most cowardly minds, let alone hearts so noble and generous as yours. I see no difficulty that your bravery cannot overcome. It is true that our enemies are many; but we are superior to them in courage, in discipline, and in arms. Besides, we have under our orders so great a number of auxiliary troops that, helped by them, we could conquer not only one but many cities equal to that of Mexico. Let it be as strong as you please; but it is not so strong that it can resist the attacks which we must give it by land and by water. Finally, God, for whose glory we fight, has declared that he will second our designs. His providence has preserved us in the midst of so many perils and disasters, has sent us new companions in place of those whom we have lost,

p. 164

and has turned to our own good those means which our enemies emBk. X. ployed for our ruin. What shall we not hope in the future from his

mercy? He is our leader in this great expedition: let us follow him,

and not render ourselves unworthy of his protection by our distrust

and pusillanimity.”

The Tlaxcalans, who strove to imitate the discipline of the

Spaniards, wished also to review their troops in the presence of

Cortés. The army began with military music of cornets, conch trum‐

pets, and other wind instruments of the kind, behind which came the

four heads of the republic armed with shield and sword, and adorned

with most beautiful plumes that rose more than two feet above their

heads. They wore their hair bound with golden ribbons, in their lips

and ears pendants of gems, and on their feet shoes of great price.

Behind them came four squires of theirs armed with bow and arrows.

After these came the four principal standards of the republic, each

with its own ensign made of feathers. Then began to pass, in well-or‐

dered files, the troops of archers, twenty abreast, the particular stan‐

dards of their companies being seen at certain intervals, each compa‐

ny composed of three or four hundred men; these were followed by

the troops armed with sword and shield, and at the end by those of

the pikemen. Herrera and Torquemada affirm that the archers were

sixty thousand, the pikemen ten thousand, and the others armed with

sword forty thousand. (b)

Xicotencatl

(b) Solís, following Bernal Díaz, as he says, counts in the review of the Tlaxcalans

no more than 10,000 men, and he censures Herrera because, departing from Bernal

Herrera does not count 80,000 men, as Solís says, but 110,000, as may be seen in his

p. 165

Xicotencatl the younger also made his speech, in imitation of Cortés, in which he told his troops that on the following day, as was well Bk. X. known to them, they were to march with the brave Spaniards against the Mexicans, their mortal enemies: that although the mere name of the Tlaxcalans sufficed to terrify all the nations of the earth, they should nonetheless strive to win new glory by their deeds.

Cortés for his part summoned the principal lords of the allied states, and exhorted them to constant fidelity toward the Spaniards, exaggerating to them the advantages which they should hope for from the ruin of the Mexicans, and the evils which they should fear if ever, through the suggestion of the Mexicans, or through fear of the war, or through inconstancy of mind, they should fail in the faith given. Then he published a military edict for the regulation of his troops, which contained the following articles:

I. Let no one blaspheme against God, nor against the Blessed Virgin, nor against the saints.

II. Let no one quarrel with another, nor lay hand to sword or other weapon to wound him.

III. Let no one gamble away his arms, nor his horse, nor his iron gear.

IV. Let no one force any woman, under penalty of death.

V. Let no one take the property of others, nor punish any Indian unless he is his slave.

VI. Let no one go out on raids without our permission.

VII. Let no one take any Indian prisoner, nor plunder his house, without our permission.

VIII. Let no one maltreat the allies; on the contrary, let every means be used to maintain their friendship.

And because it is of no use to publish laws if zeal for their observance is lacking and offenders are not punished, he had two Blacks, slaves of his, hanged because they had stolen a turkey and two cotton mantles. By these and other such punishments he made his orders respected, so necessary for the preservation of his troops.

ducive to the good outcome of his enterprise, he finally marched with

all his Spaniards and with a good number of allies on December 28,

1520, after having heard Holy Mass and invoked the Holy Spirit. He

did not then wish to take with him the whole army of allies which

had been reviewed the day before, as much on account of the difficul‐

ty there would be in maintaining so great a number of troops in

Texcoco, as because he thought it more necessary to leave the greater

part of them in Tlaxcala for the security of the brigantines, when it

should be time to transport them. (c) Of the three roads there were

for going to Texcoco, Cortés took the most difficult, prudently per‐

suading himself that since the Mexicans would not have to wait for

him on it, his march would prove safer. He set out accordingly by way

of Tetzmellocan, a village belonging to the state of Huexotzinco. On

the 30th they observed from the highest summit of those mountains

the beautiful valley of Mexico, partly with jubilation, because there

was the object of their desires, and partly with some displeasure be‐

cause of the memory of their disasters. As they began to descend to‐

ward the valley they found the road obstructed with many trunks and

branches of trees laid across it on purpose, and had need to employ a

thousand Tlaxcalans in clearing it. As soon as they reached the plain,

they were attacked by some flying squadrons of the enemy; but some

of them having been killed by the Spaniards, the others were put to

flight. That night they lodged at Coatepec, a place eight miles distant

from Texcoco, and the next day, making their way to that court, anx‐

ious about the disposition of the Texcocans, but equally resolved not

to turn back without having taken vengeance on their enemies, they

saw coming toward them four persons of authority, unarmed, one of

them with a small golden banner in his hand, and Cortés, recognizing

X. general, to invite him to betake himself to his court, and to beg him not to commit any hostility in his states; they presented to him the little banner, which had thirty-two ounces of gold. Cortés, in spite of these tokens of friendship, reproached them with the death dealt a few months before by the inhabitants of the village of Zoltepec to forty-five Spaniards, five horses, and three hundred Tlaxcalans who were accompanying them, laden with gold, silver, and arms for the Spaniards who were then in Mexico, a death carried out with such inhumanity that they had hung up as trophies in the temples of Texcoco the skins of the Spaniards with their arms and garments, and those of the horses with their shoes. He added that since it was not possible for them to make up the loss of those people, they must at least repay him the gold and silver stolen: that if they did not give him due satisfaction, for each Spaniard killed he would put to death a thousand Texcocans. The messengers replied that the Texcocans were not guilty of that, but the Mexicans, by whose orders the people of Zoltepec had done it; that nevertheless they offered to use every possible diligence that everything should be restored to them, and having taken courteous leave of the Spanish general, they returned in haste to Texcoco with the news of the speedy arrival of the Spaniards at that court.

Cortés entered Texcoco with his army on the last day of that year. Some nobles came to meet him, and led him to one of the palaces of the late King Nezahualpilli, which was so large that not only were § 2.

of that court. Not to increase the distrust of the citizens, and not to Bk. X. expose his men to some disaster, he published an edict in which, on

pain of death, he forbade his soldiers to leave their quarters without

his permission. After dining they observed from the roofs of the

palace that a great number of people were leaving the city, some mak‐

ing their way to the neighboring woods and others to various places

on the lake. The following night King Coanacochtzin himself slipped

away, betaking himself to Mexico in a boat, in defiance of Cortés,

who wished to seize him, as he had already done with his three broth‐

ers Cacamatzin, Cuicuitzcatzin, and Ixtlilxochitl. Coanacochtzin

could take no other course; for how could it be possible that he

should consider himself safe among the Spaniards after seeing what

they had done to his brothers and to King Moctezuma his uncle, and

especially since he feared that many of his own subjects might there‐

upon take occasion to declare themselves his enemies, some through

fear of the Spaniards or for the particular interest of their families,

others to avenge the death of Cuicuitzcatzin, and others again to set

Ixtlilxochitl upon the throne.

The upheavals which immediately occurred at that court suffi‐

ciently justified the decision he had taken. Cortés had been there

barely three days when the lords of Huexotla, of Coatlichan, and of

Atenco, three cities so near to Texcoco, as we have already said, that

they seemed like suburbs of that great court, presented themselves to

him, begging him to accept their alliance and friendship. Cortés, as

one who desired nothing more than to swell his party, received them

kindly and offered them his protection. The court of Mexico, as soon

as it learned of this novelty, sent those lords a severe reprimand, hav‐

ing them told that if the reason for their having adopted so base a

course was fear of the power of their enemies, they should know that

the Mexicans were in much greater strength, with which they would

shortly throw the Spaniards into confusion, together with their fa‐

vorite allies the Tlaxcalans; that if they had been reduced

by

p. 169

by concern for their states and possessions, they should move to

Bk. X. Mexico, in whose dominions much better lands would be assigned to them; but those lords, instead of being frightened by the reprimand or yielding to the promises, seized the messengers and sent them to Cortés. He asked them the reason for their embassy, and they replied that, knowing that those lords were in his favor, they had come to beg them to be willing to act as mediators for peace between the Mexicans and the Spaniards. Cortés, pretending to believe what they said, set them at liberty and charged them to tell their sovereign that he did not want war, nor would he ever wage it unless compelled by the hostility of the Mexicans; that he should therefore take heed, and guard against doing any harm to the Spaniards or to their allies, because otherwise they would behave as enemies and would infallibly ruin his cities.

The alliance of those three cities was indeed of great importance to Cortés; but more than anything else he needed to win over the court of Texcoco itself, both for the great nobility that was there and for its influence on the other cities of the kingdom. Ever since he had entered that court he had constantly sought to win hearts by courtesy and good manners, and had urged the same upon his men, strictly forbidding every kind of hostility toward the citizens. He recognized at the outset a party among the nobility favorable to Prince Ixtlilxochitl, whom he was keeping, I know not for what reason, in Tlaxcala. He had him brought to the court by a good number of Spaniards and Tlaxcalans, presented him to the nobility, and obtained that he be recognized by them as king and crowned with the same ceremonies and demonstrations of joy that they were accustomed to show toward their legitimate sovereigns. The people accepted him, either because they dared not oppose the

Spaniards, or perhaps because they were weary of the government of

Coanacochtzin. Ixtlilxochitl was a youth of about twenty-three. From

Cortés’s first entry into Tlaxcala he had openly declared for the

Spaniards, had offered himself to them with his army, and had invited

them to make the journey to Mexico by way of Otompan, where he

then was; but in spite of his good will and his services he was none‐

theless a prisoner of the Spaniards when they came out routed from

Mexico, and was kept by them in Tlaxcala until he was called to the

throne. The circumstances of this event lead us to believe that his im‐

prisonment was a decorous oppression of his liberty, colored with one

of those fine pretexts which the policy of men is wont to invent

when, on account of some distrust, they wish to put themselves in

He doubts at first, and then affirms positively, that at this same time Cacamatzin was

reigning in Texcoco, whereas we know from the testimony of almost all the histori‐

Nezahualpilli (whom he calls Nezabal), though he was his son, as all know who have

Nezahualpilli dead while the predecessor of Moctezuma was reigning. Now the pre‐

decessor of Moctezuma died in 1502; therefore Nezahualpilli was killed, at the lat‐

est, in that same year by Cacamatzin, according to what Solís says. When he had the

boldness to kill his king, he must be believed to have been at least fifteen years old;

therefore in 1519, when the aforesaid Cacamatzin visited Cortés at Ayotzinco, he

was at least 32 years old; yet Solís himself says elsewhere that he was then a youth of

25 years; but it is certain that Nezahualpilli died in 1516. 6. He supposes

Cacamatzin a usurper of the crown, when he was its legitimate heir, as is established

by history. 7. He pretends that the new king was in Texcoco when Cortés arrived

there, that Cortés had never seen him, that the first time the prince presented him‐

self to him he was so pleased with his eloquence and graciousness that, unable to re‐

strain himself, he embraced him; but all this is false; for we know from the letters of

Cortés himself, and from the histories of Herrera, Torquemada, and others, that that

prince (whose name Solís did not know) had been seen by Cortés more than a year

before, and had been his prisoner for more than six months, and that to crown him

he had him come from Tlaxcala.

p. 171

Through long familiarity with the Spaniards he grew accustomed to

Bk. X. their usages and manners. He was instructed in the Christian religion and baptized with the name of Don Fernando Cortés Ixtlilxochitl, out of regard for the Spanish general, who was his godfather. He had upon the throne nothing but some semblance of majesty; for rather than lord of his subjects he was a minister of the will of the Spaniards, to whom he rendered great services, not only in the conquest of Mexico, in which he served with his person and with his troops, but also in the rebuilding of that capital, for which he furnished some thousands of architects, masons, and laborers. He died quite young in 1523, and his brother Don Carlos, of whom we shall afterward make honorable mention, succeeded him in the lordship of Texcoco. With the elevation of Ixtlilxochitl, and with the courtesies which Cortés showed him, the Spanish party grew considerably, and all those Texcocan families that had absented themselves from the court for fear of the Spaniards’ hostilities, now rendered secure, returned gladly to their homes.

Cortés had resolved to make his quarters in Texcoco, and so had taken pains to fortify that royal palace where his troops were lodged. He could not have taken a counsel more suited to his purpose. Texcoco, as capital of the kingdom of Acolhuacan and so large a city, abounded in every sort of provisions for the sustenance of the army; it had good buildings for their lodging, good fortifications for their defense, and a plentiful supply of every kind of artisan for all the work they needed. The dominions of Texcoco, moreover, bordering on those of Tlaxcala, made the necessary communication with that republic easier for the Spaniards; the proximity of the lake was of great importance for the construction of the brigantines; and the advantageous situation of that court made the Spaniards aware of all the movements of their enemies without exposing them to their hostilities.

After putting the affairs of Texcoco in good order, Cortés resolved to make an assault on the city of Iztapalapa, to take vengeance upon it and upon its citizens for the offenses received

Spaniards, more than three thousand Tlaxcalans (e), and many

Texcocan nobles. Before reaching Iztapalapa, some troops of the ene‐

my came to meet them, pretending to oppose their entry, and fight‐

ing partly on land and partly in the water, but in fighting they kept re‐

them, entered the city, whose houses they found largely deserted, be‐

cause the citizens had taken refuge with their wives, their children,

and the greater part of their goods in the houses which they had on

the little islands of the lake; but there too they were pursued by their

enemies, who fought even in the water. The night was already far ad‐

vanced when the Spaniards, delighted with the victory which they be‐

lieved they had won, were busy plundering the houses, and the

Tlaxcalans setting fire to them; but their rejoicing was soon changed

to terror, for by the very light of the fire they observed that the water

of the canals was gushing out and beginning to flood the houses. The

danger recognized, the retreat was sounded, and the city was aban‐

doned in haste to take again the road to Texcoco; but in spite of their

diligence they came to a place where there was so much water that

the Spaniards crossed it with difficulty, and some of the Tlaxcalans

were drowned, and most of the booty was lost. Not one of them

would have remained alive, according to Cortés’s statement, if they

had stayed three hours longer in the city, because the citizens, wish‐

ing to drown all their enemies, broke the dike of the lake and flooded

the city. The next day

Otompan and of othMixquic, Otompan, and three or four other cities of those surround‐ er cities with the

Spaniards. ings gave him by means of their ambassadors, alleging, in order to obtain his favor, that, though they had been urged by the Mexicans to take up arms against the Spaniards, they had never consented. Cortés, as one who was continually acquiring greater authority the more his party grew, required of them as a necessary condition for obtaining his alliance that they should seize all the messengers who might be sent them from Mexico, and all the Mexicans who might come to their cities. They offered to do so, though not without great difficulty, and from then on were constantly faithful to the Spaniards.

This confederation was immediately followed by that of Chalco, a considerable city and state on the eastern shore of the fresh-water lake; for Cortés, knowing that the Chalcas wished to adhere to his party but dared not declare themselves for fear of the Mexican garrisons that were in their state, sent Sandoval there with twenty horse, two hundred Spanish foot, and a good number of allies, and gave him orders first to escort certain Tlaxcalan troops who wished to carry to their homeland that portion of the booty of Iztapalapa which they had saved, and then to turn upon Chalco to drive the Mexicans out of that state. Sandoval gave the vanguard to the Tlaxcalans; some Mexican troops that had put themselves in ambush rushed suddenly upon them, threw them into confusion, killed some of their men, and took the booty from them; but when the Spaniards came up they defeated

and put the Mexicans to flight. Having recovered the booty, the Bk. X. Tlaxcalans continued their journey without danger, and Sandoval

marched toward Chalco, but long before reaching the city the main

body of the Mexican garrison came to meet him, which, according to

some historians, was composed of twelve thousand men. A battle was

fought, which lasted two hours and ended with the killing of many

Mexicans and the flight of the rest. The Chalcas, informed of the vic‐

tory, came with great rejoicing to meet the Spaniards, and led them in

triumph into the city. (f) The lord of that state, dead of smallpox a lit‐

tle earlier, had in the last moments of his life warmly recommended

to the two sons he left that they should ally themselves with the

Spaniards, cultivate their friendship, and have Cortés for a father. In

consequence of his last will those two young men betook themselves

to Texcoco, accompanied by the Spanish army and by many Chalca

nobles; they presented to Cortés the value of a hundred and fifty se‐

quins in gold and established the alliance, in which they remained

constantly faithful. The reason why so many peoples of that empire

so easily rebelled was in some the fear of the Spanish arms and of the

power of their allies, and in others hatred of the Mexican domina‐

tion. It is not possible for the fidelity of subjects to be steadfast when

in their subordination terror has more influence than beneficence.

There is no more unstable throne than one that is sustained more by

force of arms than by the love of its peoples. Cortés, after showing fa‐

vor to the two young Chalcas, divided that state between them, ei‐

ther at their own request or at the suggestion of the nobility. He gave

Meanwhile the Mexicans did not cease to make raids into the states that had allied themselves with the Spaniards, but Cortés’s diligence in sending them relief rendered their efforts for the most part useless. Among others, within a few days the Chalcas themselves came to Texcoco to implore the aid of the Spaniards; for they had learned that the Mexicans were preparing to strike a great blow in that state, lately withdrawn from their dominion. Cortés could not this time comply with their requests; because, having now finished the work of the masts, the planks, and all the fittings of the brigantines, he needed his troops to have them transported safely to Texcoco. He therefore gave them the counsel to ally themselves with the Huexotzincas, the Cholulans, and the people of Cuauhquechollan. The Chalcas refused such a confederation because of their ancient enmity with those peoples; but in the end they accepted it, moved by Cortés’s urging and compelled by necessity. The Chalcas had hardly departed when three messengers from Huexotzinco and from Cuauhquechollan opportunely arrived at Texcoco, sent by those lords to Cortés to make known to him their concern on account of certain smokes, by no means obscure signs of war, observed by the sentinels which they had on the summits of the mountains, and to offer him their troops, ready at his orders, should he wish to make use of them. Cortés took advantage of so fine an occasion to confederate those states with that of Chalco, obliging them to renounce for the common good their particular resentments. This alliance was firm; from then on they helped one another mutually against the Mexicans.

Spaniards, many of them killed and others made slaves. Thence

Sandoval marched to Tlaxcala, where he found everything ready for

the transport of the worked materials of the brigantines. The first

brigantine was made by Martín López, a Spanish soldier, who served

as engineer in Cortés’s army, and was tried out in the river Zahuapan.

On its model the other twelve were built by the Tlaxcalans. The

transport was carried out with the greatest display and rejoicing of

the Tlaxcalans, who found light that burden which was to contribute

to the ruin of their enemies; eight thousand Tlaxcalans carried on

their backs the beams, the sails, and the other gear required for the

construction of the brigantines, two thousand came laden with the

provisions, and thirty thousand armed men for the defense, under the

orders of three principal chiefs, Chichimecatl, or Chichimecatecuhtli,

(*) Ayotecatl, and Teotepil, or Teotlipil. This convoy occupied, accord‐

ing to Bernal Díaz, more than six miles. When they left Tlaxcala,

Chichimecatl commanded the vanguard; but as soon as they set foot

outside the dominions of that republic, Sandoval gave him the rear

guard, because he feared some attack from the enemy. This caused a

grave displeasure to the Tlaxcalan, as one who prided himself on his

bravery, alleging that in all the battles in which until then he had tak‐

en part,

Cortés, dressed in the most sumptuous finery and accompanied by all his officers, came to meet them, and embraced and thanked those Tlaxcalan lords for their good offices. They took six hours to enter Texcoco, in the best order, crying Castile, Castile, Tlaxcala, Tlaxcala amid the din of the military instruments.

Hardly had the general Chichimecatl arrived when, without wish‐ Expeditions against

the cities of Xaltocan ing to rest from the fatigue of the journey, he begged Cortés to em‐ and Tlacopan. ploy him and his troops against the enemy. Cortés, who was waiting for nothing else than the arrival of the auxiliary troops from Tlaxcala to carry out an expedition which he had been meditating for some time, leaving in Texcoco a good garrison, and having given the proper orders concerning the completion of the brigantines, set out on the march at the beginning of spring of 1521 with twenty-five horse and six small cannon of artillery, with three hundred and fifty Spanish foot, thirty thousand Tlaxcalans, and a part of the Texcocan nobility; and because he feared that the Texcocans, whom he did not yet trust, might give some secret warning to the enemy and upset his plans, he left Texcoco without disclosing the object of his expedition. The army marched twelve miles toward the north, and spent that night in the open. The next day it went to attack Xaltocan, a strong city situated in the midst of a small lake, with a causeway leading to it that was cut, like those of Mexico, by several ditches. The Spanish infantry, aided by a good number of allies, crossed the ditches amid a dense cloud of darts, arrows, and stones, by which many were wounded; but the citizens, unable to bear longer the slaughter which the Spanish arms were making among them, abandoned the city and saved themselves by flight. The victors plundered the houses and burned some of them.

The day after, they set out for the great and beautiful city of Cuauhtitlan, as Cortés rightly calls it;

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. III. Z but

p. 178

but they found it deserted, because the citizens, frightened by what Bk. X.

had happened at Xaltocan, had sought to put themselves in safety.

Thence they passed to Tenayuca and to Azcapotzalco, and because

they met with no resistance in those three cities, they did them no

harm. Finally they arrived at the court of Tlacopan, the goal which

Cortés had set himself, in order to seek there some accommodation

with the court of Mexico, and, if that should never succeed, to inform

himself more closely of their designs and preparations. He found the

inhabitants of that city disposed to resist their entrance. These at‐

tacked the Spaniards with their usual fury, and fought courageously

for a good while; but at last, unable to withstand the fire of the mus‐

kets and the impetus of the horses, they withdrew to the city. The

Spaniards, because it was late, lodged in a great house of the suburb.

The next day the Tlaxcalans set fire to many houses of the city, and in

the six days that the Spaniards stayed there they had continual skir‐

mishes, and there were some famous duels between the Tlaxcalans

and the people of Tlacopan; but both sides fought with singular brav‐

ery, and vented the hatred they bore one another in a thousand in‐

sults. The people of Tlacopan called the Tlaxcalans the damsels of the

Spaniards, without whose protection they would never have dared to

advance as far as that city. The Tlaxcalans in turn answered them that

the title of women was owed rather to the Mexicans and to all their

partisans, since, though so superior in number to the Tlaxcalans, they

had never been able to subdue them. Nor were the Spaniards exempt

from such insults. They invited them in mockery to enter Mexico to

command there as lords and to enjoy all the pleasures of life. “Does it

seem to you, Christian,” they said to Cortés, “that things will go now

as they did the other time? Do you think that another Moctezuma

reigns in Mexico, sacrificed to your desires? Enter, enter by all means

into the court, where of all of you a great sacrifice will be made to our

gods.” In the fights which the Spaniards had in those days, they en‐

tered that fatal causeway, and

approached those memorable ditches in which nine months before they had been defeated. There they met a terrible resistance, and all Bk. X. at once were on the point of perishing; for, engaged in pursuing certain Mexican troops that had come on purpose to insult them in order to draw them into danger, they found themselves suddenly assailed on both sides of the causeway by so great a number of enemies that they retreated with difficulty, fighting furiously as far as the mainland. In this conflict five Spaniards were killed, and many wounded. Many of the Mexicans were left dead in this and in the other fights. Cortés, displeased at the ill success of his expedition, returned with his army by the same road to Texcoco, receiving on his march fresh insults from the enemy, who ascribed his retreat to fear and cowardice. (g) The Tlaxcalans who accompanied the Spaniards on that expedition, having amassed a great sum of spoils, asked Cortés for permission to carry it to their homeland, and Cortés granted it willingly. (g) Solís, wishing to give the lie to Bernal Díaz, says thus: “Whatever our historian may say to discredit this expedition (to Tlacopan), it was so important to the principal end that scarcely had Cortés returned to Texcoco when the caciques of Tucapan, of Mascalzingo, and of Auhtlan (so he calls Tuzapan, Mexicaltzinco, and Nauhtlan), and other peoples of the northern shore, came to him as suppliants to give obedience: which shows that the Spaniards returned with reputation, etc.” But setting aside the ambiguous expression northern shore, which readers unacquainted with the geography of that kingdom will perhaps understand of the shore of the lake, though it must be understood of that of the sea, and the error in saying that the lords of those places came to Texcoco, whereas we know from the testimony of Cortés that they sent their ambassadors there, it is certain that those lords could not have been induced to send such an embassy by the news of what had happened at Tlacopan, because their ambassadors arrived at Texcoco four days after that expedition, as Bernal Díaz, an eyewitness, affirms, and their cities were distant from that court more than 200 miles.

The Tlaxcalans and the other allies were frightened at the sight of the difficulty and the danger; but Sandoval, animated by that great courage which shone in all his actions, resolved to die or conquer. He began to climb with his infantry, having at the same time to overcome both the roughness of the mountain and the multitude of enemies who defended it with a cloud of darts, of stones, and even of huge rocks, which although they broke in striking against the crags in between, yet their fragments wounded the Spaniards; but nothing was able to stop them until they entered the city, bathed in sweat and in blood, and the allies did the same after their example. The fatigue and the wounds so inflamed their anger, and with such fury did they hurl themselves against the enemy, that many, fleeing from the swords, threw themselves headlong down the cliffs of the mountain. So great was the blood that was shed there that it dyed a stream that ran there, and so fouled its waters that for more than an hour the victors could not use them to appease the great thirst that tormented them. (i) “This was,” says Cortés, “one of the most signal victories, in which the Spaniards gave the greatest proofs of their courage and of their constancy.” This day cost the life of Gonzalo Domínguez, one of the bravest soldiers of Cortés, whose loss was very keenly felt by all.

p. 183

sovereign that he claimed nothing else than that the King of Spain

Bk. X. should be recognized as lord of that land, in accordance with what had been agreed by the Mexican nobility in that respectable assembly which was held in Mexico before King Moctezuma; that they should remember the homage which all the Mexican lords then paid to the great monarch of the East; that he wished to establish a firm peace and an eternal alliance with them, and made war only when compelled by their hostilities; that it grieved him to have to shed so much Mexican blood and to destroy such great and beautiful cities; that they themselves were witnesses of the bravery of the Spaniards, of the superiority of their arms, of the multitude of their allies, and of the good fortune of their progress; that they should at last come to their senses, and not oblige him by their obstinacy to continue the war to the total ruin of the court and of the empire.

The fruit of this embassy was recognized at once in the complaints of the Chalcas, who, aware of the great forces that were being assembled against their state, came to implore the aid of the Spaniards, presenting to Cortés, painted on a cloth, the cities that were arming by order of the king of Mexico against them, and the route they were to take. While Cortés was making ready his troops for that expedition, there arrived at Texcoco the messengers of Tuzapan, of Mexicaltzinco, and of Nauhtlan, cities situated on the coast of the § 9. Mexican gulf on the other side of the colony of Veracruz, to give, in March of the Spanish

army through the the name of their lords, obedience to the King of Spain. southern mountains.

darts and stones when he called a retreat; for besides perceiving that

the undertaking was rash, and more difficult than fruitful, an army of

enemies was seen that was marching toward that quarter with the in‐

tent of assailing the Spaniards from behind while they should be most

engaged in the assault. Cortés went to meet them with his troops

well ordered. The battle lasted little, because the enemy, recognizing

themselves inferior in force, quickly abandoned the field. The

Spaniards pursued them for more than an hour and a half until they

had completely routed them. The loss of the Spaniards in this battle

was almost nothing; but in the assault on the mountain eight of them

were killed, and many wounded. (m)

He began, then, to climb with the utmost toil; but when he reached a post as high as that where the enemy were, he saw the Spanish banner raised on one of the crags or cavaliers. The enemy, finding themselves attacked on two sides, and having already begun to feel the damage which the firearms did them, surrendered. Cortés received them with singular humanity; but required of them, as a necessary condition for obtaining pardon, that they should induce likewise to surrender those who occupied the first mountain, as in fact happened.

§ 10. Huaxtepec, Yauhtepec, and Xiuhtepec for the great and pleasant city Conquest of of Cuauhnahuac, (*) capital of the Tlahuica nation, more than thirty Cuauhnahuac. miles distant from Mexico toward the south. This city was very strong by its situation; for on one side it was surrounded by precipitous mountains, and on the other by a ravine about seven perches deep, where a stream ran. Cavalry could not enter it except by two roads, unknown at that time to the Spaniards, or by the bridges, if they had not been raised, when they appeared there.

While they were looking for a suitable place to make the assault, the Bk. X.

people of Cuauhnahuac shot at them an indescribable quantity of ar‐

branches, made of them a bridge to pass to the other side; his exam‐

ple was soon imitated, though with great difficulty and in great peril,

by six Spanish soldiers, and then by many others, both Spaniards and

Tlaxcalans. (n) Such intrepidity so frightened those who on that side

were defending the entrance into the city that they at once withdrew

and went to join the other citizens, who on the other side of the city

were opposing the troops led by Cortés; but when they were most en‐

gaged in the defense, they found themselves suddenly assailed by

those troops which, behind that courageous Tlaxcalan, had already

entered the city by the undefended side. Terror drew the citizens

back from the defense, and put them to headlong flight toward the

mountains; so that the allies burned without any opposition a good

part of the city. The lord of it, who had fled with the others, fearing

to be overtaken in the mountains by the Spaniards, took the resolu‐

tion to surrender, protesting that he had not done so before because

he was waiting for the anger of the Spaniards to be spent in the city,

and, satisfied with other hostilities, to refrain from raging against his

person.
