Bk. X. the city of Xochimilco. This beautiful city, the largest after the courts of all those of the Mexican valley, was founded on the shore of the § 11. lake of Chalco, little more than twelve miles from the capital. Its Conquest of

Xochimilco. population was very numerous, its temples many, its buildings magnificent, and singularly beautiful its gardens floating on the lake, whence it had the name of Xochimilco. (*) Like the capital, it had many canals, or ditches, and now, for fear of the Spaniards, they had made many entrenchments. As soon as they saw the army coming, they raised the bridges from the canals to make the entrance more difficult. The Spaniards divided the army into three squadrons to assail the city from as many sides; but everywhere they found great resistance, and could not storm the first ditch except after a terrible fight of more than half an hour, in which two Spaniards were killed and many wounded; but having at last overcome these obstacles, they entered the city, pressing hard upon the Xochimilcas, who from the boats in which they had taken refuge continued fighting until night. There were heard at times among the combatants certain voices asking for peace, but the Spaniards, being convinced that such voices were aimed at no other end than to gain time to put their families and goods in safety, and to receive the relief they were expecting from Mexico, pressed them harder, until, finding no resistance, they withdrew to rest and tend the wounded; but they had hardly begun to breathe when they saw themselves assailed by a great number of enemies, who came in battle order by that same street by which the Spaniards had entered. These were reduced to the greatest straits, and Cortés himself ran great danger of remaining a prisoner of the enemy; for his horse having fallen from exhaustion, as he says, or been thrown to the ground by the blows of the Xochimilcas, as other historians affirm, he continued fighting

on foot with his lance; but, overwhelmed by the enemy, he could not Bk. X.

Spaniards had leisure to rest somewhat from the toils of the day, in

their general himself, and the principal captains, Alvarado and Olid.

Four Spaniards taken prisoner were conducted to the capital, and

without delay sacrificed, and their arms and legs sent to various places

to encourage the subjects against the enemies of the state. There is

no doubt that on this as on many other occasions Cortés could easily

have been killed by his enemies, if they had not had that foolish ea‐

gerness to take him alive to sacrifice him to their gods.

The news of the capture of Xochimilco threw the court of Mexico

into great consternation. King Cuauhtémoc summoned some military

chiefs, and set before them the damage and the danger caused to

Mexico by the loss of so considerable a place, the service they would

render to the gods and to the nation by recovering it, and the courage

and the forces needed to defeat those bold and pernicious strangers.

Orders were therefore given at once to arm an army of twelve thou‐

sand men to be sent by land, and another to be sent by water; and

this was carried out with such promptness that the Spaniards had

scarcely rested from the fatigue of the day before when Cortés was

warned by his sentinels of the march

The Mexican commanders came full of pride, displaying certain European swords taken from the Spaniards in the defeat of July 1. The battle was fought outside the city, and when it seemed good to Cortés he ordered the troop posted on the hillock to assail the Mexicans from behind. These, seeing themselves attacked on every side, fell into confusion and fled, leaving five hundred dead on the field. The Spaniards, returned to their quarters, learned that the troop left there had been in great danger because of the multitude of Xochimilcas who had fought it. Cortés, after having been three days in Xochimilco in frequent fights with the enemy, had fire set to the temples and houses, and went to the market, which was outside the city, to order his men for the march. The Xochimilcas, persuading themselves that their departure was an effect of fear, attacked the rear guard with loud cries; but they were so thoroughly beaten by the Spaniards that they did not dare to assail them again.

He found the city deserted, and the next day went out from it to reconnoiter the road that led from that city as far as the causeway of Iztapalapa. He found there an entrenchment made by the Mexicans, and ordered the infantry to attack it, and in spite of the terrible resistance of the enemy who defended it they stormed it, ten Spaniards being wounded and some Mexicans killed. flicted by such a misfortune; but his grief was increased when from the

upper court of the great temple of that court he contemplated, togeth‐

er with other Spaniards, that fatal causeway on which some months be‐

fore he had lost so many of his friends and soldiers, and considered at‐

tentively the great difficulties that must be overcome in order to make

himself master of the capital. Some suggested to him that he should

send his troops along that causeway to commit some act of hostility

against the Mexicans; but he did not wish to expose them to so great a

risk, and so, without remaining longer in that city, he returned by way

of Tenayuca, Cuauhtitlan, Citlaltepec, and Acolman to Texcoco, having

in this journey gone around all the lakes of the Mexican valley, and ob‐

served all that was needed in order to carry out successfully the great

enterprise which he was meditating.

§ 13. In Texcoco Cortés continued all the preparations for the siege. The Conspiracy against

completed, sufficiently deep, and on either side furnished with piles to

receive the water of the lake, into which the brigantines were to be

launched, and a machine built for launching them. (p) The troops

which Cortés had under his orders were innumerable, and the number

with those who a few days before had come from Spain in a ship that

Bk. X. put in at the port of Veracruz, laden with horses, arms, and munitions of war. Everything seemed to point to a happy outcome, when the enterprise fell into the greatest danger of being ruined. Certain Spanish soldiers, partisans of the governor of Cuba, moved by hatred of Cortés, or by envy of his glory, or, what seems more likely, by fear of the perils that awaited them in the siege of the capital, agreed secretly to take the life of Cortés, of his captains Alvarado, Sandoval, and Tapia, and of all those whom they saw more attached to the party of that general. The conspirators had not only determined the time and the manner of carrying out the blow safely, but had even chosen those on whom the vacant offices of general, judge, and captains were to be conferred; but one of the accomplices, repenting of his crime, opportunely revealed the treachery to Cortés. The general immediately had Antonio de Villafaña, the head of the conspiracy, seized and committed the examination of the culprit to a judge; and when the culprit frankly confessed his crime, he was hanged by sentence of justice from a window of the quarters. Toward the accomplices Cortés prudently dissembled, pretending not to believe them guilty and attributing to the malice of Villafaña the infamy that resulted against them from his confession; but so that in the future his life might not be so exposed to such dangers, he created a bodyguard composed of several soldiers, of whose fidelity and courage he was well assured, who accompanied him by day and by night and kept constant watch over the preservation of his person.

The conspiracy having thus been prevented by the punishment of § 14. its principal culprit, Cortés applied himself with greater activity to Last preparations for

the siege of Mexico. giving the last touch to his great enterprise. On April 28, after the Mass of the Holy Spirit had been celebrated, at which all the Spaniards received communion, and after the brigantines had been blessed by a priest, these were launched into the water, and, spreading their sails at once, began

to

p. 192

to sail over the lake to the firing of the artillery and muskets, which Bk. X.

was followed by the singing of the Te Deum with the music of the mil‐

itary instruments. All these demonstrations were owing to the great

confidence that Cortés had in the brigantines for the success of his

enterprise, without which, perhaps, he could never have brought it to

a good end. He then held a review of his army, and found in it eighty-

six horse and more than eight hundred Spanish foot, three large iron

cannon, fifteen smaller ones of copper, a thousand Castilian pounds

of musket powder, and a great quantity of balls and of arrows, the

number and the strength of his little army having been doubled by

the reinforcements that had come to him that year from Spain and

from the islands of the Antilles. To encourage them he made them an

address like the one he had already made when he left Tlaxcala. He

sent messengers to this republic, to Cholula, to Huexotzinco, and to

other cities, letting them know that the work on the brigantines was

already finished and begging them to send him within ten days as

many picked troops as they could, since the time had now come to

lay siege to that proud court, which for so many years had oppressed

their liberty. Five days before the feast of Pentecost the army of

Tlaxcala arrived at Texcoco, consisting, according to Cortés himself,

of more than fifty thousand men, under the command of several fa‐

mous chiefs, among whom came the younger Xicotencatl and the

valiant Chichimecatl, whom Cortés went out to meet with his men.

The troops of Huexotzinco and of Cholula passed there through the

mountains of Chalco, according to the order given them. In the two

following days other troops came from Tlaxcala and from other

neighboring places, which together with those mentioned made more

than two hundred thousand men, according to the testimony of their

leader Alonso de Ojeda.

On Whit Monday (May 20) Cortés assembled his men in the main

square of that court, to make the division of the army, to name the

commanders, and to assign to each of them the place where he was to

pitch

his

Cristóbal de Olid was named master of the camp and head of the division destined for the city of Coyoacán, and there were assigned to him thirty-three horse, a hundred and sixty-eight Spanish foot, under three other captains, with two cannon, and more than twenty-five thousand allies. To Gonzalo de Sandoval were given twenty-four horse, a hundred and sixty-three Spanish foot under two captains with two cannon, and the allies of Chalco, Huexotzinco, and Cholula, who numbered more than thirty thousand; and Cortés ordered him to go first to ruin the city of Iztapalapa, and then to encamp in whatever place should seem to him most suitable for the purpose of pressing the Mexicans. Cortés, in spite of the remonstrances made to him by his captains and soldiers, took command of the brigantines, since he judged his own assistance more necessary in them. He distributed among the thirteen brigantines three hundred and twenty-five Spaniards and thirteen falconets, assigning to each brigantine a captain, twelve soldiers, and as many rowers; so that the whole army destined to begin the siege of the capital consisted of nine hundred and seventeen Spaniards and more than seventy-five thousand men of auxiliary troops (q), whose number shortly after

or in a place near it, as Bernal Díaz affirms, a crier having first published the cause of his sentence, which was that of having deserted Bk. X. and of having stirred up the Tlaxcalans against the Spaniards. It is to be believed that Cortés did not risk the execution of such a punishment without having first obtained, as Herrera expressly affirms, the consent of the senate of Tlaxcala; which was not difficult, considering their severity in punishing crimes even in the most conspicuous persons, and the particular hatred they bore that prince, whose pride they could no longer endure. So resounding a punishment, which should naturally have stirred up the minds of the Tlaxcalans against the Spaniards, so terrified both them and the other allies that from then on they observed the laws of military service more punctually and kept themselves more subordinate to those foreign leaders, the Spaniards drawing profit even from their own faults; but the Tlaxcalans did not fear to make some demonstrations of esteem and veneration toward their prince, weeping for his death, distributing among themselves his garments as precious relics, and celebrating, as is to be believed, his obsequies with due magnificence. The family and goods of Xicotencatl were adjudged to the King of Spain and carried to Texcoco. In the family there were thirty wives, and among the goods a great quantity of gold.

that Xicotencatl was put to death in Texcoco: “because it would have been too great a risk for Cortés to resolve upon so violent an execution under the eyes of so great a number of Tlaxcalans, to whom the ignominious punishment inflicted on one of the foremost men of their nation must necessarily have been distressing;” but Cortés risked far more in imprisoning King Moctezuma in his own court, and under the eyes of an incomparably greater number of Mexicans, to whom that great affront done to the first man of their nation must without fail have been distressing. (s) Cortés landed there, and overcoming with a hundred and fifty men the roughness and difficulty of the ascent and the resistance of the enemy, he took the hill by storm, with the killing of all who defended it. (t) But hardly had they taken it when they saw a great fleet of boats (u) coming against them, summoned by the signals of the smoke that at the first appearance of the brigantines had been made both on that hill and in some temples of those surroundings. The Spaniards embarked at once and stood on the defensive without moving until, aided by a strong wind that opportunely arose, and increasing the speed of the brigantines by the impulse of the oars, they dashed against the boats, breaking some and capsizing others by the shock. Some of the enemy perished wounded by the balls, and many were drowned. All the other boats fled, pursued for more than eight miles by the brigantines as far as the capital.

The commander Olid, as soon as he saw from a temple of Coyoacán the fight of the brigantines, marched with his troops in battle order along the causeway that led to Mexico, took by storm some ditches and entrenchments, and killed many of the enemy. Cortés for his part that evening gathered his brigantines,

camps of Coyoacán and of Tlacopan was of great importance to

Cortés, in order to give his orders promptly and to bring aid wherever

it was needed. Finally, the nearness to Mexico helped to facilitate the

camp, and fifty picked foot from the troops of Sandoval. That night a

great crowd of enemies was heard coming toward that camp. The

Spaniards, knowing that the Mexicans were not accustomed to fight

at night unless they were sure of victory, were terrified at first; but al‐

The next day they found themselves assailed by a prodigious multitude of warriors, who with frightful shouts magnified their number in the imagination of the Spaniards. Cortés, the relief that he was awaiting from Coyoacán having opportunely arrived, made a sortie with his men drawn up in battle order. They fought on both sides with great courage and obstinacy; but the Spaniards and their allies took a ditch and an entrenchment, and with the artillery and the horses did the Mexicans so much harm that they obliged them to take refuge in the city; and because on the side of the lake that lay to the west of the causeway they were troubled by the Mexican boats, Cortés had a ditch of the causeway widened so that the brigantines could pass through it, and these hurled themselves impetuously against them, pursued them as far as the city, and set fire to some houses of the suburbs.

Meanwhile Sandoval, having happily completed, though not without great risk, the expedition of Iztapalapa, marched with his troops toward Coyoacán. On the road he was attacked by the troops of Mexicaltzinco; but he defeated them, and had fire set to their city. Cortés, aware of his march and of a great ditch that had recently been made in the causeway, sent him two brigantines to facilitate the passage of the army. The latter marched toward Coyoacán, and Sandoval went with ten horse to the camp of Cortés. When he arrived there he found those Spaniards in combat with the Mexicans. The fatigue of the journey and of the battle of Mexicaltzinco did not suffice to keep him from the fray. He fought as usual with courage, but in the fighting his leg was pierced by a dart, and along with him many other Spaniards were also wounded. These advantages gained by the Mexicans are not to be compared with the loss that they suffered that day, nor with the fear that the fire of the artillery caused them, which was so great that for many days they did not dare to approach the camp of the Spaniards. These spent six days in continual

fights

Alvarado for his part pressed the Mexicans as hard as he could,

taking in frequent skirmishes some ditches and entrenchments of the

causeway of Tlacopan; but some of his men were killed and many

wounded. He observed that by the causeway of Tepeyacac, situated

toward the north, relief was continually introduced into the city, and

he perceived that by that causeway escape would be easy for the be‐

sieged, whenever they should find themselves unable to resist the be‐

siegers any longer. He promptly made this known to Cortés, and the

latter commanded Sandoval to go with a hundred and eighteen

Spanish foot and a very great number of allies to occupy that place,

and thereby prevent the relief that came to the enemy. Sandoval

obeyed, though still suffering from the wound in his leg, and took

possession of that place without opposition, every communication by

land with the other cities being from then on cut off for the

Mexicans. (*)

number fewer than eighty thousand men. Cortés marched along his

Bk. X. causeway with his numerous army well ordered and flanked by the brigantines, and having scarcely gone a little way he came upon a wide and deep ditch and an entrenchment more than ten feet high. The Mexicans courageously opposed their passage, but, driven back by the artillery of the brigantines, the Spaniards passed on, pressing them as far as the city, where they found another great ditch and a strong and high entrenchment. The force of the water in this ditch, the throng of enemies who had gathered to the defense, their frightful and threatening cries, and the thick rain of arrows, darts, and stones that they shot, held up for some time the resolution of the Spaniards; but having finally, with the discharge of all the artillery and of all the firearms, driven from the entrenchment those who defended it, the army passed over it, and advanced taking other ditches and entrenchments as far as a principal square of the city, which was full of people. In spite of the slaughter that they saw being made in the multitude by a great cannon planted at the entrance of the square, the Spaniards did not dare to enter it, until the general himself, reproaching them for that ignominious fear, and hurling himself intrepidly against the enemy, put courage into his soldiers. The Mexicans, terrified by so great an intrepidity, fled into the enclosure of the great temple, and seeing themselves still assailed there, took refuge in the upper courts of the temples, where they were likewise pursued; but suddenly the Spaniards found themselves attacked from behind by other Mexican troops, and put in such a strait that, being unable to sustain the fury of the enemy either within the enclosure of the temple or outside in the neighboring square, they were forced to retire to the causeway by which they had entered the city, leaving the cannon of artillery in the power of the enemy. Shortly after, three or four horse arrived opportunely in the square, and the Mexicans, persuaded that all the cavalry was coming against them, were thrown into confusion by the fear they had of those great and fiery beasts, and abandoned ignominiously

Bk. X. divided among the camps of Sandoval and of Alvarado. This aid from the Texcocans was soon followed by the confederation of the Xochimilcas and of the mountain Otomis with the Spaniards, which new troops increased Cortés’s army by twenty thousand men.

Nothing more was wanting to this general for the completion of the siege except to prevent the relief that was being introduced into the city by water. Keeping therefore seven brigantines, he sent the other six toward that part of the lake that lay between Tlacopan and Tepeyacac, so that from there they could easily give aid to the camps of Sandoval and of Alvarado, whenever those commanders should require it; and while not employed by them, should go cruising two by two, and try to capture all the boats that carried provisions or troops to the city.

Cortés, now finding himself with so great a number of allied troops, determined to make within three days an entry into the city. He gave therefore the necessary orders, and on the appointed day marched with the greater part of his cavalry, with three hundred Spanish foot, with seven brigantines, and with an innumerable multitude of allies. They found the ditches opened, the entrenchments rebuilt, and the enemy well prepared to resist; nevertheless, with the aid of the brigantines they took all the ditches and entrenchments that were there as far as the principal square of Tenochtitlan. There the army halted, Cortés not permitting them to advance further without leaving leveled all the difficult passes that he had taken; but while ten thousand allies were occupied in filling the ditches, the others applied themselves to burning and ruining some temples, houses, and palaces, and among others that of King Axayacatl, where the Spaniards had formerly had their quarters, and the celebrated palace of the birds of King Moctezuma. After these hostile acts, accomplished with great difficulty and with grave danger because of the efforts the Mexicans made to prevent them, Cortés sounded the retreat, which was carried out happily,

Cc2 though

p. 204

Bk. X.

though the rear guard was incessantly harassed by the enemy troops.

Alvarado and Sandoval did the same on their part. This day was with‐

out doubt one of great fatigue for the Spaniards and their allies, but

also of unspeakable grief for the Mexicans, as much for the loss of so

many fine buildings as for the taunts with which they were insulted

by their own vassals confederated with the Spaniards, and by the

Tlaxcalans, their mortal enemies, who in fighting showed them the

Cortés sallied from his camp in the same manner as the day before;

but in spite of his diligence the Mexicans had already rebuilt the

greater part of the destroyed fortifications, and defended them with

such obstinacy that the army of the besiegers could not take them ex‐

cept after a furious fight of five hours. The army advanced and took

two ditches of the causeway of Tlacopan; but as the day was now

about to end, it withdrew to its camp, still fighting the enemy troops,

who were pressing upon the rear guard. The same combats that

Cortés’s army had were had by those of Sandoval and of Alvarado, the

besieged having to fight at one and the same time against three most

numerous armies, superior to them in arms, in horses, in brigantines,

and in military discipline. Alvarado on his part had already ruined all

the houses that stood on either side of the causeway of Tlacopan; (A)

The inhabitants of the cities sit‐ with the Spaniards. uated on the shore and on the islets of the lake of Chalco had until then been enemies of the Spaniards, and could have done much harm to Cortés’s camp if their troops had assailed it from one side of the causeway at the same time that the Mexicans assailed it from the other; but they had attempted no hostility against the Spaniards, perhaps because they reserved it for a more opportune occasion. The Chalcas and other allies, to whom the neighborhood of so many enemies was not convenient, tried to draw them to their party, now with promises and now with threats and vexations, and so much did their importunity avail, and perhaps also the fear of vengeance from the Spaniards, that there came to Cortés’s camp to confederate with him the nobles of Iztapalapa, of Mexicaltzinco, of Colhuacan, of Huitzilopochco, of Mixquic, and of Cuitláhuac, which cities were a considerable part of the Mexican valley. Cortés rejoiced infinitely at that alliance, and asked of them not only that they should help him with their troops and their boats, but also that they should bring materials for making huts along that causeway, because, it being the rainy season, his men suffered too much from lack of shelter.

All this was so well carried out by them that they immediately placed Bk. X. under Cortés’s orders a considerable body of troops, whose number is

four miles distant from Xoloc; and, not content with such great aid,

they brought to Cortés’s camp many provisions, and chiefly fish and

cherries in great quantity.

Cortés, finding himself thus with his forces so increased, entered

the city with them two or three days in succession, making a consid‐

erable slaughter of the citizens. He persuaded himself that the be‐

sieged must surrender, seeing themselves faced with so excessive a

number of troops and experiencing the pernicious effects of their ob‐

stinate resistance; but he was deceived, for the Mexicans were re‐

solved to lose their lives before their liberty. He resolved, however, to

make continual entries into the city in order to compel them by in‐

cessant hostilities to ask for the peace that they refused. He formed

of his vessels two flotillas, each composed of three brigantines and of

fifteen hundred boats, ordering them to approach the city, to set fire

to the houses, and to do the Mexicans all the harm possible. He or‐

dered Sandoval and Alvarado to do the same on their part, and he

himself, with all his Spaniards and, as it seems, with eighty thousand

allies (B), marched as usual along the causeway of Iztapalapa toward

the city, without being able to obtain, either on this or on other en‐

tries of those days, any other advantages than that of gradually dimin‐

ishing the number of the enemy, ruining some buildings,

He had likewise taken some ditches and entrenchments, and knowing that the greatest strength of the enemy was in Tlatelolco, where King Cuauhtémoc resided, and where an infinite number of people from Tenochtitlan had taken refuge, he directed his operations toward that quarter; but although he fought with all his forces by land and by water, he could not advance as far as he wished, because of the stubborn opposition of the besieged, in which fights many perished on both sides.

In one of the first skirmishes a sturdy and courageous Tlatelolca showed himself, disguised as an Otomi, with an Ichcahuepilli, or cotton cuirass, and without other arms than a shield and three stones, and running very swiftly toward the besiegers he hurled the three stones in succession with such dexterity and such force that with each one he struck down a Spaniard, causing no less indignation to the Spaniards than fear and wonder to their confederates. They used every diligence to get him into their hands; but they could never catch him, because in all the fights he appeared differently dressed, and in all did great harm to the besiegers, having moreover as much speed in his feet to save himself as strength in his arms to attack. The name of this celebrated Tlatelolca was Tzilacatzin.

Alvarado, puffed up by some advantages gained over the Mexicans, wished one day to advance as far as the market square: he had already taken some entrenchments and some ditches, and among others one that was more than fifty feet wide and more than seven deep, and, forgetting in his good fortune to have it filled up, as his general had ordered him, he advanced with forty or fifty Spaniards and some allies.

hurled themselves against them with such fury that almost all the

It was now twenty days that the Spaniards had been making con‐

§ 24. tinual entries into the city; whence some captains and soldiers, weary Victory of the

Mexicans. of so many fights, the fruit of which they still saw far off, complained to the general and entreated him to venture all the great forces he had on one decisive stroke, which would finally draw them out of so many perils and hardships. Their design was to advance as far as the center of Tlatelolco, where the Mexicans had gathered their forces, in order to ruin them utterly in a single day, or at least to induce them to surrender. Cortés, who knew very well how dangerous such an enterprise was, tried to dissuade them from it with the most effective arguments; but, nothing availing, and being unable besides to resist a counsel that had become almost general, he yielded at last to their importunate entreaties. He ordered the commander Sandoval to go with a hundred and fifteen foot and ten horse to join Alvarado, to place the cavalry in ambush and have the baggage removed, feigning to depart and to abandon the siege of the city, so that the Mexicans, lured into pursuing them, should be attacked from behind by the cavalry; that with the six brigantines he should try to make himself master of that great ditch where Alvarado had been defeated, having it filled and leveled; that he should not take a step forward without leaving the way well prepared for the retreat, and that he should strive to enter the market square by force of arms.

On the appointed day for the general assault Cortés marched with twenty-five horse, with all his infantry, and with more than a hundred thousand allies. His brigantines, with more than three thousand auxiliary boats, formed the wings of his army on either side of the causeway. He entered the city without any opposition, and at once divided his army into three parts, so that by as many streets they might arrive at the same hour at the market square. The command of the first division was given to Julián de Alderete, the king’s treasurer, who

Cortés, who with the diligence proper to a good general had come to the ditch when the routed troops arrived there, tried to stop them with his shouts, so that they should not by their disorder facilitate the slaughter begun to be made by the enemy; but what voices are capable of restraining the flight of a confused multitude, to which fear lends speed? Pierced by the keenest grief for the loss of his men, and heedless of his own danger, he approached the ditch to rescue those he could. Some came out unarmed, others wounded, and others half drowned. He tried to put them in order and to start them toward the camp, remaining himself behind with twelve or twenty men to guard their rear; but he had scarcely begun to march when he found himself in a narrow pass surrounded by the enemy. That day would have been his last, in spite of the extraordinary courage with which he defended himself, and together with his life all hope of the conquest of Mexico would have been lost, if the Mexicans, instead of giving him death, as they easily could, had not been intent on taking him alive, to honor with so illustrious a victim their gods. They had already taken him, and were leading him without delay to the sacrifice, if his men, warned of his captivity, had not promptly come to free him. Cortés owed his life and his liberty chiefly to a soldier of his guard called Cristóbal de Olea, a man of great courage and singular skill in arms, (*) who on another occasion had delivered him from a like danger, and in this one saved him at the cost of his own life, cutting off with a stroke of his sword the arm of that Mexican who had seized him. Cortés was also indebted for his liberty to the prince Don Carlos Ixtlilxochitl, and to a brave Tlaxcalan called Temacatzin.

At length the Spaniards got out, though with great difficulty and Bk. X.

with not a few wounds, onto the wide causeway of Tlacopan, where

Cortés was able to put them in order, taking the rear guard himself

with the cavalry; but the boldness and fury with which the Mexicans

pursued them were such that it seemed to them impossible to escape

with their lives. Those who had entered by the other two streets had

had terrible fights; but because they had been more diligent in stop‐

ping up the ditches, their retreat was less difficult, when Cortés gave

them the order to march to the great square of Tenochtitlan, where

they assembled. From there they saw, to their very great distress, ris‐

ing from the braziers of the great temple the smoke of the copal that

the Mexicans burned in thanksgiving for the victory won; but their

grief increased further when they saw some heads of Spaniards

thrown toward them by the Mexicans to dishearten them, and heard

it said that the commanders Alvarado and Sandoval had been killed.

From the square they took the causeway of Iztapalapa to their camp,

pressed continually by a great multitude of enemies.

Alvarado and Sandoval had striven to enter the market square by a

street that led from that of Tlacopan to Tlatelolco, and had happily

advanced their operations as far as a post not far from that square;

but having seen the sacrifices of some Spaniards, and heard from the

Mexicans that Cortés and his captains had been killed, they retired

with the utmost difficulty; for to those enemies who had been fight‐

ing them before were added those who had defeated Cortés’s troops.

The loss that the besiegers suffered that day was seven horse,

many arms and boats, and a cannon of artillery, more than a thousand

allies, and more than sixty Spaniards, partly killed in the battle and

partly taken prisoner and immediately sacrificed in the great temple

of Tlatelolco in sight of Alvarado’s men. The captain of a brigantine

was also killed. Cortés received a wound

The Mexicans celebrated the victory for eight continuous days with illuminations and music in the temples; they made the fame fly through the whole kingdom, and had the heads of the slain Spaniards carried through the provinces to frighten those that had rebelled against the crown and to recall them to their obedience, as in fact they obtained it from some. They dug out the ditches anew, repaired the entrenchments, and restored the city, except the temples and the houses ruined by the enemy, to the state in which it had been before the siege began.

Meanwhile the Spaniards stood on the defensive in their camps, §Fights25.

of the brigantending their wounds and refreshing themselves for the fights to tines, and stratagems come; but lest the Mexicans should take advantage of their inactivity of the Mexicans. to introduce provisions into the city, Cortés ordered the brigantines to go cruising two by two about the lake. The Mexicans, recognizing the superiority of the Spanish vessels and arms, and not being able to employ equal arms, tried at least in some way to match the brigantines. They had therefore built thirty large boats, called by the Spaniards piraguas, well equipped and covered with thick planking, so as to be able to fight from them without so much risk of being damaged. They determined to lay with them an ambush for the brigantines among the thickets of reeds that were in the floating clumps in the lake, and they planted in several places thick stakes hidden under the water, so that the brigantines, striking against them, might break, or at least be hampered in their defense. The ambush being arranged, they made three or four ordinary boats come out from the reed beds of the lake, so that, by provoking two brigantines that were cruising there,

where they lay in ambush, prepared an ambush of his own of six brigantines,

and, profiting by the enemy's example, ordered that one brigantine should ap‐

hidden. It all happened as Cortés had planned; for the Mexicans, seeing the

brigantine, promptly came out of ambush, and just when they thought them‐

selves surest of their prey, the other five brigantines came out against them

and at once began to play their artillery, with whose first discharge they over‐

turned some boats and threw the rest into disorder. The greater part of the

Mexicans perished, and some were taken prisoner, among them some nobles,

whom Cortés wished to use in order to press for some accommodation with

the court of Mexico.

§ 26. He therefore sent word to King Cuauhtémoc, by means of these noble A fruitless embassy prisoners, that he should consider how much the forces of Mexico were di‐

to the king of minishing at the very time in which those of the Spaniards were increasing;

Mexico. that in the end they would have to yield to the greater power; that even if the

Spaniards did not enter that court to commit hostilities, it would suffice

merely to cut off their relief to make them die

of

p. 215

they

of hunger; that they could still avoid the disasters that threatened them; that if they consented to the proposals of peace, he would at Bk. X. once cause all hostilities to cease, the king would remain in peaceful possession of the crown, with all the greatness, all the power, and all the authority which he had hitherto enjoyed, and his subjects would remain free and masters of all their goods, without his requiring anything more from His Majesty or from his subjects than the homage due to the King of Spain, as supreme lord of that empire, whose rights had already been recognized by the Mexicans themselves, as resting on the ancient tradition of their ancestors; that if on the contrary he persisted in the war, he would be deprived of the crown, the greater part of his vassals would lose their lives, and that fine and great city would be utterly ruined. The king consulted his councilors, the generals of the army, and the heads of religion; he laid before them the subject of the embassy, the state of that court, the scarcity of provisions, the affliction of the people, and the still greater evils that threatened them, and commanded them to say freely what they thought. Some, foreseeing the outcome of the war, inclined toward peace; others, spurred by hatred of the Spaniards or by the incentive of honor, advised war. The priests, whose authority in this matter as in others was held in the highest respect, strongly opposed peace, alleging several pretended oracles of their gods, whose anger was to be feared if they yielded to the demands of those cruel enemies of their worship, and whose protection had to be sought with prayers and sacrifices. This opinion prevailed in the end, through the superstitious fear that had taken possession of their minds, and in accordance with it the Spanish general was answered that he should continue the war, since they had resolved to defend themselves to their last breath. Had they been brought to such a resolution not by superstition but by honor, by love of their country and of their own liberty, they would not have been so blameworthy, because although they foresaw their ruin as inevitable by continuing the war,

p. 216

Bk. X.

they had no hope of improving their fortune by peace. The experi‐

ence of past events did not allow them to trust the promises that

were made to them, so that it had to seem to them more consonant

with their ideas of honor to die with arms in hand in defense of their

country and of liberty than to abandon that same country to the am‐

bition of those strangers, and to reduce themselves by their surrender

to a sad and wretched servitude.

Malinalco, who, as these messengers asserted, wished to ally them‐

selves with the Cohuixcas, a rather numerous nation, in order to de‐

stroy Cuauhnahuac, because it had allied itself with the Spaniards,

and then to cross the mountains and come with a great army to at‐

tack Cortés's camp. This general, although he was in a position to ask

for help rather than to give it, nevertheless, for the reputation of the

Spanish arms, and to forestall the blow with which he was threatened,

sent Captain Andrés de Tapia with the messengers, with two hundred

Spanish foot, ten horse, and a good number of allies, giving him or‐

ders to unite with the troops of Cuauhnahuac and to do whatever he

should judge conducive to the service of his king and to the safety of

the Spaniards. Tapia carried out what was enjoined upon him by his

general, and in a small place situated between Cuauhnahuac and

Malinalco he fought a great battle with the enemy, routed them, and

pursued them to the foot of the high mountain on which stood the

city of Malinalco. He could not assault it, as he would have liked, be‐

cause the mountain was inaccessible to cavalry; but he laid waste the

countryside, and since the term of ten days prescribed to him was

nearly complete, he returned to Cortés's camp.

Two days later there arrived at the same camp the messengers of

the Otomis of the Valley of Toluca, asking help against the

Matlatzincas, a powerful and warlike nation of the

same

p. 217

same valley, who were making continual war upon them, had burned one of their towns and had taken many of them prisoner, and who, Bk. X. besides this, had agreed with the Mexicans to attack Cortés's camp with all their forces from the mainland side at the same time as the Mexicans should attack it from the side of the city. On entering Mexico, the Spaniards had sometimes heard the Mexicans threaten them with the power of the Matlatzincas, and Cortés now perceived, from the report of the Otomis, the great danger he would run if he gave the enemy time to carry out their design. He would entrust this important expedition to no one but the brave and invincible Sandoval. This tireless man, although he had been wounded on the day of Cortés's defeat, had in the days that followed acted as general, going ceaselessly around all three camps and giving the most opportune orders for their security. Scarcely fourteen days had passed since the defeat when he marched toward the Valley of Toluca with eighteen horse, a hundred Spanish foot, and sixty thousand allies. On the way they saw some signs of the slaughter made there by the Matlatzincas, and when they entered the valley they found a place freshly ruined, and saw the enemy troops going laden with spoils, which they abandoned as soon as the Spaniards appeared, wishing to be less encumbered for the battle. They crossed a river that traverses the valley, and remained on its bank to await the Spaniards. Sandoval crossed it intrepidly with his army, attacked the enemy, put them to flight, and pursued them for a good nine miles to a city where they took refuge, leaving more than a thousand Matlatzincas dead on the field. Sandoval besieged the city and forced the enemy to abandon it and to take shelter in a fortress built on the top of a steep mountain. The victorious army entered the city, and after sacking it they set fire to the buildings; and because it was late and the troops were too weary, the assault on the fortress was put off until the next day, when, expecting to have

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. III. Ee a

a great struggle, they found the fortress empty. Sandoval resolved to Bk. X.

pass, on his return, through some places that had likewise declared

themselves hostile; but he had no need to use arms against them, for,

frightened at seeing so great an army come against them, swelled with

numerous Otomi troops, they surrendered at once. Sandoval received

them with the utmost kindness, and asked of them that they induce

the Matlatzinca nation to make friendship with the Spaniards, point‐

ing out to them the advantages they could hope to have from them

and, on the contrary, the evils that enmity would bring upon them.

These expeditions were of the greatest importance; for four days af‐

ter Sandoval had returned there arrived at Cortés's camp several lords

of the Matlatzincas, of Malinalco, and of the Cohuixcas (E) to ask

pardon for their hostilities and to establish a confederation, which

was as useful to the Spaniards as it was harmful to the Mexicans.

The Spaniards no longer had enemies to fear on the side of the

mainland, and Cortés had at his disposal so excessive a number of

troops that he could have employed in the siege of Mexico many

more people than Xerxes sent against Greece, had the situation of

that capital not made so great a multitude of besiegers rather an en‐

cumbrance. The Mexicans, on the contrary, found themselves aban‐

doned by their friends and by their subjects, surrounded by enemies,

and afflicted by famine. That unfortunate court had against it, besides

the Spaniards, the kingdom of Acolhuacan, the republics of Tlaxcala,

of Huexotzinco, and of Cholula, almost all the cities of the Valley of

Mexico, and the very numerous nations of the Totonacs, the Mixtecs,

the Otomis, the Tlahuicas, the Cohuixcas, the Matlatzincas, and oth‐

ers; so that

Chichimecatl. that the Spaniards after the defeat were keeping on the defensive, resolved to make an entry into Mexico with his Tlaxcalans alone. He left, then, the camp of Alvarado, where he had always been since the beginning of the siege, accompanying the Spaniards in all the fights and ever making his bravery conspicuous. This time he carried all the ditches that were on the causeway of Tlacopan, and, leaving four hundred archers on guard at the most dangerous one, so that they might secure the passage on the retreat, he entered the city with the main body of his troops, where he had a terrible fight with the Mexicans, in which many were killed and wounded on both sides. The Mexicans flattered themselves that they could defeat him at the passage of the ditch, and pressed him hard in his retreat; but thanks to the archers posted on the other bank of the ditch, he crossed back happily with his Tlaxcalans, and returned full of glory to their camp. (F)

Ee2 The

(F) Bernal Díaz says that after the defeat the Spaniards were left abandoned by their allies, who, out of the fear that the Mexicans put into them with certain threats which they made them in the name of their gods, all went away to their homes; that in Cortés's camp there remained no one but the prince Don Carlos with forty Texcocans; in Sandoval's, a lord of Huexotzinco with fifty men; and in Alvarado's, General Chichimecatl with eighty Tlaxcalans. But this cannot be, because two days after that defeat Captain Tapia set out from Cortés's camp to make war on the people of Malinalco, and took many allies with him, as the same Bernal Díaz affirms. Twelve days after Tapia, Commander Sandoval set out from the same camp with sixty thousand allies, as Cortés says. At the time when Sandoval was making war on the Matlatzincas, that is, sixteen or eighteen days after the defeat, General Chichimecatl made his famous entry, and he could not have made it without many thousands of Tlaxcalans. It is certain that not all the allies went away, and that if some did go away they returned at once, because within a few days there was in the three camps, and especially in Cortés's, a number of them far greater than there had been before the defeat. Cortés says nothing of such a desertion, and it is not very likely that he would have omitted it in recounting his misfortunes to the king.

The Mexicans, to avenge the boldness of the Tlaxcalans, assailed Bk. X.

Alvarado's camp one night; but having been heard in time by the sen‐

tinels, the Spaniards and the allies ran to arms. The fight lasted three

hours, during which time Cortés, having heard a cannon shot from

his camp, and suspecting what it in fact was, thought this a fitting oc‐

casion to make an entry into the city with his men, who were by now

healed of their wounds. The Mexicans who had gone to Tlacopan, be‐

ing unable to overcome the resistance of the Spaniards, returned to

the city, where they found Cortés's army; they fought with ardor, but

without any considerable advantage on either side.

At this same time, when the need of arms and munitions was

greatest, a ship put in at Veracruz that brought the Spaniards a fresh

relief, with which they were placed in a position to continue their op‐

erations. Prince Don Carlos Ixtlilxochitl had advised the Spanish gen‐

eral not to engage in new assaults, in which his army would have to

suffer too much; that without exposing himself to so many ills, and

without ruining the beautiful buildings of that capital, he could make

himself master of it merely by preventing the entry of provisions; for

the greater the number of the besieged, the sooner they would con‐

sume the few provisions they had. This wise counsel, all the more

valuable as it was the less to be expected from a prince who by his age

and his intrepidity longed for occasions to make his courage shine,

pleased Cortés so much that, unable to restrain himself, he ran to em‐

brace him, signifying his gratitude to him in singular expressions. He

held to such counsel for some days; but then, weary of the length of

the siege, he returned to the former hostilities; but before continuing

them he offered peace anew to the Mexicans, making them a compar‐

ison of the forces of the one side and of the other, and repeating the

reasons which he had already on another occasion proposed to them.

The Mexicans replied that they would never lay down their arms until

the Spaniards should go away to their own country.

Now

p. 221

Now Cortés, seeing the resolution of the Mexicans, after forty-five

Bk. X. days of siege, and that the more he invited them to peace the more obstinately they rejected it, determined not to advance a step farther § 29. into the city without destroying all the buildings that stood on one Slaughter in Mexico,

and the bravery of side and the other of the street, both to avoid the harm which the flat some women. roofs did to his troops, and to constrain the besieged by such hostilities to accept his proposals. He therefore solicited, and obtained from the allied lords, some thousands of peasant laborers provided with the necessary tools for demolishing the buildings and filling the ditches. In the following days he made several entries into the city with his Spaniards and brigantines, and with more than a hundred and fifty thousand allies, destroying buildings, filling ditches, and diminishing by slaughter the number of his enemies, though not without grave danger to his own person and to his men; for he very nearly was taken prisoner, and would have been had his soldiers not come to his aid, and his troops were at times obliged to withdraw by flight from the fury of the Mexicans; some Spaniards and allies perished there; and two brigantines were already almost overwhelmed by a fleet of canoes, but another brigantine that came up drew them out of the danger.

The bravery of some Spanish women (*) became famous in these entries; they had voluntarily accompanied their husbands to the war, and by the continual hardships they suffered, and by the examples of valor which they had ever before their eyes, they had become soldiers. They stood guard, they marched with their husbands armed with cotton corselets, with buckler and sword, and they threw themselves intrepidly into the midst of the enemy, increasing, in spite of their sex, the number of the besiegers.

On July 24 a new entry was made into the city

surrounded by ditches. Of the four parts of the city three remained

that day in the power of the Spaniards, the besieged having been re‐

duced to the part of Tlatelolco, which, because there was more water

there, was the stronger and the safer.

From a Mexican lady taken in the last assault the Spanish general

learned the miserable state of the city, owing to the want of provi‐

sions and the discord that existed among the besieged; for the king,

his relatives, and many nobles had determined to die rather than sur‐

render, but the people were disheartened and weary of the siege. This

was confirmed by two deserters of middling rank, who, driven by

hunger, came to Cortés's camp.

On these tidings Cortés resolved to let no day pass without enter‐

ing the city until he should carry it or ruin it; so he returned there on

the 25th with his army, and carried a broad street in which there was

a ditch so great that in the whole day it could not be filled or stopped

up. They demolished or burned all the houses that were in that part,

in spite of the resistance of the enemy. The Mexicans, seeing the al‐

lies employed in tearing down the buildings, said to them: “Ruin

them, you traitors, ruin those houses, for afterwards you will have the

labor of rebuilding them.” “We,” the allies answered, “shall surely re‐

build them, if you are the victors;

but if you are vanquished, you yourselves will rebuild them, so that

In the entry made there on the 26th, two great ditches which the Mexicans had newly made were carried. Alvarado for his part was advancing every day farther into the city; and on the 27th he went so far, carrying several ditches and entrenchments, that he came to occupy two towers near the palace where King Cuauhtémoc resided; but he could not thence pass farther, as he wished, because of the extreme difficulty he found in the ditches and the vigorous resistance of the enemy, who forced him to retire, pressing furiously upon his rear guard. Cortés, having observed an extraordinary smoke that rose from those towers, and suspecting what had in fact happened, entered the city, as he was accustomed to do, and spent the whole day in repairing all the bad passages. He now lacked no more than one canal and one entrenchment to enter the market square. He determined to advance as far as that, as in fact he accomplished, and that was the first time since the siege began that his troops joined with those of Alvarado, with unspeakable joy on both sides. Cortés entered that square with some cavalry, and found there an innumerable people lodged in the porticoes, because they could not be contained in the houses of that ward. He climbed the temple, from which he observed the city, and saw that of its eight parts only one remained to be carried. He had fire set to the high and beautiful towers of that temple, in which, as in the greater one of Tenochtitlan, the idol of the god of war was worshiped. The Mexican commoners, on seeing that great flame, which seemed to rise to heaven, raised a great lament. Cortés, moved to pity at seeing so numerous a people

reduced

order to Commander Alvarado to advance by force of arms along a

great street where there were more than a thousand houses, and he

himself with all his army went to assail them from another side. So

great was the slaughter which they made that day among the besieged

that, between dead and prisoners, there were more than twelve thou‐

sand. The allies were so cruel against those unhappy victims that they

spared them neither for age nor for sex, the severe orders of the gen‐

eral not sufficing to restrain their cruelty.

The next day Cortés returned with all his forces; but he command‐

ed that no harm should be done to the besieged, both for the com‐

passion which the sight of their miseries caused him, and for the

hope he had of inducing them to surrender. The Mexicans, seeing so

great a number of troops coming against them, and among them their

own subjects, who had formerly served them and now threatened

them with death, finding themselves now reduced to such misery and

calamity, and having before their eyes so many lamentable objects, for

they

p. 225

they had nowhere even to set foot except upon the corpses of their fellow citizens, they vented their rage in horrible cries, and demanded Bk. X. death as the only remedy for their ills. Some commoners begged Cortés to confer with certain nobles who were defending an entrenchment, in order to treat of some accommodation. Cortés went to them, although he had little hope of success. These were precisely some of those who could no longer bear the hardships of the siege; so that when they saw the Spanish general approach them, moved by despair they said to him: “If you are the son of the Sun, as some imagine, why, when your Father is so swift that in the brief space of a day he completes his course, do you take so long to free us from so many ills by death? We wish indeed to die, in order to go to heaven, where our god Huitzilopochtli awaits us to give us rest from our labors and the reward of our services.” Cortés proposed to them various reasons to move them to surrender; but as they answered that this was neither in their power nor had they any hope of being able to persuade the king, he withdrew, in order to urge him by means of a distinguished personage whom he had made prisoner three days before, and who was an uncle of the king of Texcoco. That man, although wounded, was charged with betaking himself to Tlatelolco to treat of that business with the king; but no other fruit of his embassy was seen than the reiterated outcries with which the people demanded death. (H) Some Mexican troops hurled themselves desperately against the Spaniards, but they were so weakened by hunger that the harm they did was slight, and too great that which they received from their enemies.

the most valuable thing. The ambassadors, after having made known

at the meal the great need they suffered, went away, and two hours

later returned, bringing Cortés a gift of very fine garments which the

king sent him, and repeating the aforesaid excuses. Three days passed

in these negotiations without any fruit.

p. 227

Cortés had given orders to the allies to stay outside the city, be‐

Bk. X. cause the Mexicans had begged him not to permit them to take part when the conference with the king should be held; but now, having § 32. lost all hope of an accommodation, he recalled all the troops of his A terrible conflict,

and horrible slaughcamp, in which there were more than a hundred and fifty thousand ter of the Mexicans. men, and those also of Alvarado's camp, and with all these forces together he set himself to attack certain ditches and entrenchments which were the greatest fortifications that remained to the Mexicans, and at the same hour Sandoval with his army assailed the city from the north side. This was the most disastrous day for that city, on which Mexican blood was shed more copiously than ever, those unhappy citizens having by now neither arms to repel the multitude and the fury of their enemies, nor strength to defend themselves, nor even ground to fight upon. The soil of the city was covered with corpses, and the water of the ditches and of the canals was tinged with blood. Nothing was seen but ruin and slaughter, nor was anything heard but lamentable weeping and cries of despair. The allies raged so cruelly against that wretched people that the Spaniards labored more to restrain their cruelty than to fight with their enemies. The slaughter that was made of the Mexicans on that day was so great that between dead and prisoners, by what Cortés himself says, it exceeded forty thousand persons.

The intolerable stench of so many unburied corpses then forced § 33. the besiegers to withdraw from the city; but the next day (August 13) Last assault, and capthey returned to it to give the last assault to that corner of Tlatelolco ture of the city and

but even by the desire to make himself master of the treasures of the

king and of the nobility; for if that last part of the city were taken by

assault, the Mexicans, deprived of every hope of saving their riches,

might throw them into the lake, in order that the victors should not

enjoy them, and in case they did not do so, they would be seized by

the allies, who, being innumerable and more familiar with the houses,

would leave little or nothing to the Spaniards in the disorder and con‐

fusion of the assault. He therefore spoke again, from an eminent

place, to certain Mexicans of rank who were well known to him, rep‐

resenting to them their extreme danger, and begging them to make

new entreaties to the king, that he should yield to that conference so

much desired by him for the good of the king himself and of all his

subjects; for if he still persisted in his resolve to defend himself, he

was determined not to leave a single Mexican alive that day. Two of

those nobles undertook to persuade the king, but they had scarcely

departed when they returned, accompanying the Cihuacoatl, or

supreme magistrate of the court. He was received by Cortés with sin‐

by which he seemed to wish to show his spirit superior to all calami‐

soliciting a conference with my king and lord Cuauhtémoc. He is re‐

much such a resolution grieves me; but there is no remedy. Take,

then, whatever counsel pleases you best, and act according to your

designs.” Cortés told him to go and prepare

the minds of his citizens for the death that they would soon have to

Bk. X. suffer.

In the meantime numerous troops of women, of children, and of wretched people had come to give themselves up to the Spaniards, hastening in rivalry to escape from that extreme peril, and some of those who strove to cross the ditches by swimming were drowned, through their great weakness. Cortés commanded that no harm should be done to those who surrendered; indeed he stationed at several posts some Spaniards, in order that by their authority they should restrain the barbarous cruelty of the allies; but in spite of his orders more than fifteen thousand men, women, and children perished at the hands of those inhuman and furious troops.

The nobles and the military men who had held obstinately to the resolution of defending themselves to the last breath occupied the flat roofs of the houses and some paved streets. Cortés, seeing that it was late and that they would not surrender, had the artillery play upon them, and this not sufficing, he gave the signal for the assault with an arquebus shot. The besiegers all assailed at one hour, and pressed the weak and afflicted citizens so hard that, there being left to them in the city no place where they could defend themselves from the fury of so great a number of enemies, many threw themselves into the water, and others came to give themselves up to the victors. The Mexicans had made ready some boats in order to escape by flight from the power of the Spaniards; but Cortés, having perceived such a subterfuge, had given orders to Sandoval to take possession with the brigantines of the harbor of Tlatelolco and to seize the boats. In spite of Sandoval's extreme diligence many boats escaped, and among others precisely the one that carried the royal persons. That active commander, having noticed it, ordered García Holguín, captain of the swiftest brigantine, to give chase to it; he made such haste that in a short time he overtook them, and as the Spaniards made ready to fire upon the fugitives, these stopped their oars and threw down their arms as a sign of surrender. There were on that great boat, or piragua, the king of

Mexico

p. 230

Mexico, Cuauhtémoc, the queen Tecuichpotzin his wife, the king of Bk. X.

Acolhuacan Coanacochtzin, and the king of Tlacopan

Tetlepanquetzaltzin, with other personages. The brigantine came

alongside, and the king of Mexico, advancing toward the Spaniards,

said to that captain: “I am, O Captain, your prisoner, nor do I ask any

other favor of you than that you show to the queen my wife and to

her maidens that regard which is due to their sex and to their rank”;

and taking the queen by the hand, he passed with her into the brigan‐

tine. Then, observing that the Spanish captain was anxious about the

other boats, he told him not to doubt that all, as soon as they should

know that their sovereign was a prisoner, would come to die with

him.

Captain Holguín conducted those illustrious prisoners to Cortés,

who was then on the flat roof of a house in Tlatelolco. He received

them with every demonstration of honor and humanity, and had

them sit down. Cuauhtémoc, with great spirit, said to him: “I have

done, O valiant General, both for my own defense and for that of my

subjects all that the honor of my crown and zeal for my people re‐

quired of me; but because the gods were against me, I now see myself

deprived of the crown and of liberty. I am your prisoner; dispose of

my person at your pleasure”; and placing his right hand upon a dagger

which Cortés wore at his belt, he added: “Take from me with this

dagger a life that I did not lose in the defense of my kingdom.”

Cortés strove to console him with kind reasons, protesting to him

that he did not consider him his prisoner, but the prisoner of the

greatest monarch of Europe, in whose clemency he ought to trust

that not only the liberty which he had unhappily lost, but also the

throne of his most illustrious ancestors, which he had so worthily oc‐

cupied and defended, would be restored to him. But what comfort

could he have from such protestations, or what faith could he give to

the words of Cortés, he who had always been his enemy, having seen

that to King Moctezuma it was not enough to be his

friend

p. 231

allies

friend and protector to avoid losing his crown, his liberty, and his life? He demanded of Cortés that no harm be done to his subjects, and Cortés in turn de‐ Bk. X. manded of him that he command all his subjects to surrender. Each gave his orders, and both were promptly obeyed. It was likewise ordered that all the Mexicans should leave the city without arms and without baggage, and, according to what a most truthful eyewitness affirms, (*) for three days and three nights all three causeways were seen full of men, women, and children, weak, emaciated, and filthy, who were leaving by them to take refuge in other places. The stench that so many thousands of putrefied and unburied bodies gave off was so intolerable that it caused some indisposition to the general of the Conquerors. The houses, the streets, and the canals were full of disfigured corpses; (I) the ground of the city was found dug up in some parts by the citizens, who sought roots under the earth to feed upon, and many trees were seen stripped of their bark to meet the extremity of hunger. The general had the corpses buried, and an immense quantity of wood burned throughout the city, both to purify that infected air and to celebrate his victory.

The news of the capture of the capital having spread at once throughout the land, the provinces of the empire gave obedience to Cortés, although there were some which even two years afterward troubled the Spaniards with war. The

(*) Bernal Díaz del Castillo.

(I) “It is true, and I swear it, amen, that the whole lagoon, and the houses, and the barbacoas were full of bodies and heads of dead men, so that I do not know in what manner to write it; for in the streets, and in the very courts of Tlatelolco, there was nothing else, and we could not walk except among bodies and heads of dead Indians. I have read of the destruction of Jerusalem; but whether in that there was such mortality as in this, I do not know,” etc. Bernal Díaz in ch. 156 of his history. Such expressions from an eyewitness so truthful, and one who did not know how to exaggerate, give some idea of that horrible slaughter. I suspect that the corpses were left unburied by the Mexicans on purpose, to drive off the besiegers with the stench; nor can I persuade myself otherwise, considering the extreme solicitude of those nations in the obsequies of their dead.

p. 232

allies returned to their countries exceedingly joyful over the booty, Bk. X.

and content to have overthrown that court whose domination they

could not endure, or whose arms kept them in perpetual disquiet,

without perceiving that with their own arms they were forging the

chains that were to strangle their liberty, and that, that empire once

ruined, all the other nations were bound to be abased.

The booty was much less than the victors had hoped. The clothing

that was found there was divided for the most part among the allies.

Those works of gold, silver, and feathers that were kept entire on ac‐

count of their singular artistry were sent as a gift to the Emperor

Charles V. All the rest of the gold, which was melted down, scarcely

amounted to 19,200 ounces, (K) both because the Mexicans threw

the greater part of it into the lake, (L) and because individuals,

Spaniards as well as their allies, in sacking the city sought to recom‐

pense themselves secretly for their labors.

The capture of that great court took place on August 13, 1521, a

hundred and ninety-six years after it was founded by the Aztecs, and

a hundred and sixty-nine years after it was raised into a monarchy,

which eleven kings ruled. The siege of Mexico, comparable in its mis‐

fortunes and in its slaughter with that of Jerusalem, lasted seventy-

five days, in which time several thousand of the more than two hun‐

dred thousand allies perished, and more than a hundred of the nine

hundred Spaniards. The number of the Mexicans who died is not

known; but in view of the account of Cortés and of Bernal

As for those who died of hunger, or of sickness caused by the brackish water which they drank, or by the infection of the air, Cortés himself affirms that they were more than fifty thousand. The city remained almost wholly ruined. The king of Mexico, notwithstanding the magnificent promises of the Spanish general, was after a few days ignominiously put to the torture (which he endured with unconquered constancy) in order that he should declare where the immense riches of the court and of the temples were; (M) and three years later he was hanged, on certain suspicions, together with the kings of Texcoco and of Tlacopan. (N) The Mexicans, with all

Ancient History of Mexico, Vol. III. Gg the

(M) The torture that was given to King Cuauhtémoc was that of burning his feet little by little after anointing them with oil. A close private friend of his accompanied him, and died in the torments. Bernal Díaz adds that the king of Tlacopan was also tormented along with him. Cortés, against his will, took up so unworthy and barbarous a resolution in order to yield to the urgings of some greedy Spaniards, who suspected that he did not wish to put the king to the torture in order to profit secretly by the whole royal treasure.

(N) Cuauhtémoc, king of Mexico, Coanacochtzin, king of Acolhuacan, and Tetlepanquetzaltzin, king of Tlacopan, were hanged from a tree by sentence of Cortés in Izancanac, capital city of the province of Acallan, on one of the three days preceding Lent of the year 1525. The cause of their death was a certain conversation which they had among themselves about their misfortunes, in which they suggested how easy it would be for them, if they wished, to kill Cortés and all the Spaniards, and to recover their liberty and their crowns. A Mexican traitor, to win the favor of the Spanish general, gave him an account of everything, altering the sense of the words, and representing as an organized conspiracy what had been merely talk in the air. Cortés, who was then journeying toward the province of Comayagua with a few Spaniards weakened by fatigue, and with more than three thousand Mexicans whom he was leading with him, became convinced that there was no other remedy for avoiding the danger by which he believed himself threatened, except that of putting the three kings to death. This execution, says Bernal Díaz, was most unjust, and blamed by all of us who traveled with him that day. It caused Cortés great melancholy and some sleepless nights. The same author adds that Father Juan de Varillas, a friar of the Order of Our Lady of Mercy, confessed and comforted them at the execution; that they were good Christians, and that they died well disposed; whence it is manifest that they had been baptized; but among so many historians of Mexico there is not one who makes mention of an event so notable and so glorious as that of the baptism of these three kings, while otherwise filling so many pages and so many

p. 234

DESCENDANTS

the nations that contributed to their ruin remained, in spite of the Bk. X. Christian and most humane dispositions of the Catholic Kings, aban‐

doned to misery, to oppression, and to the contempt not only of the

Spaniards, but even of the lowest African slaves and of their infamous

descendants, God punishing in the miserable posterity of those na‐

tions the injustice, the cruelty, and the superstition of their ancestors:

a dreadful example of divine justice, and of the instability of the king‐

doms of the earth.

End of the Third Volume.

leaves of trifles; and what is worse, Torquemada, who labored twenty years on the

history of Mexico, and swelled his three stupendous volumes with accounts of the

discovery of the Solomon Islands, of the revolutions of the Philippines, of the perse‐

cution in Japan, and of a thousand other irrelevant things, makes no mention at all of

the conversion of those kings.

From that most noble couple, whom we have placed under number VI, were born four sons, Diego, Ferdinando, Antonio, and Fabrizio, and as many daughters, Rosa, Maria Teresa, Stefania, and Caterina. 1. Don Diego was the heir of the marquisate of the Valle, or of the Vaglio, as they say in Naples, and of the duchies of Monteleone and of Terranova. 2. Don Ferdinando married Donna Lucrezia Pignatelli, Princess of Strongoli, whose son Don Salvatore took Donna Giulia Mastrilli, of the Dukes of Marigliano. 3. Don Antonio married in Spain the only daughter of the Count of Fuentes. From that marriage was born Don Gioacchino Pignatelli d'Aragon, Moncayo, etc., Count of Fuentes, Marquis of Coscojuela, Grandee of Spain, Prince of the Holy Roman Empire, Knight of the Golden Fleece and of Santiago, etc., Ambassador of the King of Spain at the courts of England and of France, and President of the Royal Council of the Military Orders, whose son Don Luigi, now living, has married the only daughter and heiress of Casimiro Pignatelli, Count of Egmont, Duke of Bisaccia, etc., Knight of the Fleece, and Lieutenant General of the Armies of the Most Christian King. 4. Don Fabrizio took to wife Virginia Pignatelli, sister of the Princess of Strongoli, whose son Don Michele is Marquis of Salice and Guagnano. 5. Rosa was given in marriage to the Prince of Scalea. 6. Maria Teresa to the Marquis of Westerloo, a Bohemian lord. 7. Stefania to the Prince of Bisignano. 8. Caterina to the Count of Acerra.

M I S TA K E S OV E R L O O K E D I N T H E F I R S T T WO

VO L U M E S .

In vol. I, p. 126, where it is said, speaking of the migration of the Toltecs, that it began in the year I Tecpatl, that is, in 596 of the common era, it should read thus: in the year I Tecpatl, that is, in 544 of the common era, as is found in my Spanish original and in my dissertations; the other was a mistake in the translation. On the same page, where it is said that the monarchy of the Toltecs began in the year VIII Acatl, that is, in 667 of the common era, it should say in the year VII Acatl, etc., since the year 667 was not VIII, but VII Acatl. In vol. 2, p. 59, where it is said that every four years the Mexican year was advanced by one day on account of the intercalary day of our leap year; so that in the last years of the Mexican century it began on February 14, etc., it should say, it began on February 13.

On p. 16 of the same vol. 2, where it is said that three festivals were held for the goddess Centeotl in the third, eighth, and eleventh months, it should say, in the fourth, eighth, and eleventh months, as is seen in the account of the festivals, p. 68, and in the Calendar, p. 236.

In the aforesaid Calendar, p. 239, where one reads Festival of Maculitochtli, it should say, Festival of Macuiltochtli, and at the end of the Calendar, where it says, The following year II Acatl begins with II Mizquili, it should say, with II Miquiztli.

T H E P R I N T E R’ S N O T I C E .

The volume of the dissertations will probably not be published until the month of August of this year (1781), but every possible effort will be made so that it may come to light sooner.
